#962 shipped the guard anchored on the most recent **`unlabeled`** event for `needs-human-review` — "is the resolution comment newer than the last time the gate came off?" That reads as the right question and is not: `/shipyard:resolve-decisions`' [Record + unblock](./resolve-decisions.md#record--unblock) sequence posts the decisions comment **first** (step 1) and removes the gate label **second** (step 2). A genuine resolution comment is therefore *always* a few seconds **older** than the unlabel event it itself caused, so `RESOLUTION_AT > LAST_UNGATE_AT` was structurally unsatisfiable on the exact flow the guard exists to protect. The guard was inert from the day it shipped — present, tested for its text, and never firing.

The repro in [#1557](https://github.com/mattsears18/shipyard/issues/1557), measured against `mattsears18/lightwork#4619`, shows the four-second gap doing the damage:

| time (UTC) | event |
|---|---|
| `2026-08-26T06:14:06Z` | `needs-human-review` **applied** (the gate the decisions answer) |
| `2026-09-04T01:39:57Z` | `<!-- shipyard-resolve-decisions -->` comment posted — all four decisions answered |
| `2026-09-04T01:40:01Z` | `needs-human-review` **removed** (4s later, same `/my-turn` run) |
| `2026-09-04T01:53:39Z` | `/do-work` scope-preflight **re-applies** `needs-human-review` |

Under the `unlabeled` anchor: `01:39:57 > 01:40:01` is false → guard silent → re-gate lands. Under the `labeled` anchor: `2026-09-04T01:39:57Z > 2026-08-26T06:14:06Z` is true → guard fires → the defer must name what changed or is rejected. Anchoring on the gate's **application** also keeps the other direction honest: a genuinely new gate applied *after* a decision was recorded postdates `RESOLUTION_AT`, so the guard correctly stays silent and the new escalation proceeds.

The same issue fixed a second, independent over-fire in the opposite direction: a **partial** `/resolve-decisions` run posts the *same* `<!-- shipyard-resolve-decisions -->` sentinel (headed `## Decisions resolved (partial — N of M)`) and deliberately leaves the gate on, because a blocking decision is still unanswered. Every sentinel consumer — this guard, [`decision-freshness-check.md`](../skills/worker-preamble/decision-freshness-check.md), and `classify-blocked-bail.sh`'s #1279 check — matched it as a full resolution, which would suppress a gate the maintainer explicitly chose to keep. All three now exclude the partial heading. A partial-only thread still defers, but its `reason` must cite the specific decisions the partial comment lists as outstanding rather than re-asking the whole set.

## Step 6 — Scope-result freshness check (issue #563)

Without a freshness check, the orchestrator re-dispatches a scope agent against an issue even when a marker-tagged diagnosis comment already documents the same conclusion — burning a fresh scope-agent invocation to re-derive something already on the issue. The repro that motivated this: a maintainer bulk-cleared `needs-human-review` labels across a batch of issues while the underlying diagnosis comments (1–4 days old, still accurate) remained posted on them. The next session's scope pre-flight, seeing the label gone, re-dispatched scope agents against all of them — 7 fresh agents returned nearly verbatim re-derivations of the existing comments, at a cost of roughly 330k wasted tokens. The freshness check (a configurable `scope.diagnosis_reuse_hours` window, default 72h) closes this: before dispatching, check for a recent marker-tagged comment whose conclusion the body and comment thread haven't since invalidated, and if it's still fresh, reuse it as a `cached-diagnosis` defer instead of re-scoping.

## Step 6 — C=1 inline scope pre-flight must read the comment thread and check for merged PRs (issue #1162)

The C=1 "just-in-time" note only ever specified *when* the top candidate's scope decision runs (inline, right before dispatch) and *who* could make it (the orchestrator itself, without a separate scoping `Agent` call) — it never explicitly said the mechanical checks "the rest of step 6's mechanics" already require still apply when the orchestrator takes that inline path. The gap showed up in exactly the shape you'd expect: a live session scoped an issue inline — read the body, re-measured live label counts, composed a dispatch prompt — without reading the candidate's comment thread or checking for a merged PR that referenced it. The dispatched worker's own step-2 pre-implementation read caught it immediately: a PR had merged 29h earlier that already shipped part of the fix and committed the phase-2 gate criteria to a spec file — a fact invisible anywhere else in the dispatch path, since the step-4 backlog filter's `closingIssuesReferences` join only looks at **open** PRs. Cost: ~160k tokens for a dispatch a single `gh issue view --json comments` call at scope time would have prevented, plus a dispatch prompt that asserted a confidently-wrong "shippable now" framing derived from the body alone — the exact failure mode [#781](https://github.com/mattsears18/shipyard/issues/781) already warns against, reached here through the scope path instead of the prompt-composition path #781 was written for.

The fix keeps the inline path (it's still cheaper than a subagent dispatch for one candidate) but makes the two signals a scope agent would have read explicitly mandatory before the dispatch prompt is composed: the comment thread (already required by #781's grounding rule for any confidence claim, now stated at the point where that claim actually gets composed) and the staleness probe's existing merged-PR check (`gh pr list --state merged --search "<N> in:body"`), reused rather than duplicated. Neither is a hard defer trigger on its own — matching [#1148](https://github.com/mattsears18/shipyard/issues/1148)/[#1154](https://github.com/mattsears18/shipyard/issues/1154)'s posture, a hit is a reason to look and narrow the prompt, not an automatic park, since a false defer silently drops real work.

## Step 6 — Design, architecture, epic, and spike decisions are in-scope (issue #767 repro)

This is the scope-preflight-side extension of [RATIONALE → Don't defer an epic, spike, or design-needed issue for open design decisions (#767)](#dont-defer-an-epic-spike-or-design-needed-issue-for-open-design-decisions-issue-767) — the same rule, one level earlier, applied to whether to dispatch an issue at all rather than to an in-worker implementation choice. The gap it closes: a scope agent (or a human reading a stale mental model of the taxonomy) could reasonably read the pre-#767 `human-decision-required` class description ("needs a product / design / legal call") as license to defer any issue whose body merely says "needs a design" or "open questions on architecture." The repro: in one session, three freshly-filed design/spike issues were parked as `needs-triage` under exactly that reading, until the maintainer manually overrode it and pointed out none of the three needed a human — the agent could have made the design call itself and shipped a phase-1 slice.

## `SHIPYARD_REPO_ROOT` — per-call-site re-derivation kept over a step-0.4 config snapshot (issue #1105, follow-up to #1059/#1064)

**Decision: keep option (a) — the current per-call-site re-derivation shape (`SHIPYARD_REPO_ROOT` re-derived and exported from the `.shipyard-primary-root` stash at each post-relocation `shipyard-config.sh` call site) — over option (b), a one-time config snapshot at step 0.4 that every later read would consume instead of shelling out again.** #1059/#1063 introduced the pin and its first consumer; #1064 swept the then-known remaining call sites and added [`scripts/tests/shipyard-repo-root-preamble.test.sh`](../scripts/tests/shipyard-repo-root-preamble.test.sh) as the regression guard; #1059's own body deferred the snapshot-at-0.4 alternative as an explicit open design question rather than deciding it. #1105 is that decision.

**Why (a) over (b):**

- **The residual risk #1105 named turned out to be live, not hypothetical, and (a) has a cheap, complete fix for it.** Before deciding anything, this PR audited every `$CLAUDE_PLUGIN_ROOT/scripts/shipyard-config.sh"` / `resolve-dispatch-model.sh"` call site across `commands/do-work/` (mechanically, not by re-reading #1064's list) and found one the hand-maintained `FILES` array had already missed: `setup/01b-backlog-overview.md`'s step-2 `triage.investigate_dispatch` read calls `shipyard-config.sh` post-relocation with no `SHIPYARD_REPO_ROOT` pin, and was never added to #1064's six-file array (that file grew the call after #1064's sweep, or was simply missed). This is exactly the class of bug the pin exists to prevent — a repo with `.shipyard/config.local.json` set would silently lose that config layer for this one read, no error. Both halves of the fix are in this PR: the missing pin was added to the offending block, and the regression test's `FILES` array was converted from a hardcoded list to the same mechanical `find`-based discovery `scripts/tests/claude-plugin-root-preamble.test.sh` already uses for the analogous `$CLAUDE_PLUGIN_ROOT` preamble (every `*.md` under `commands/do-work/`, recursively, minus `setup/00-config-worktree.md`, which is excluded explicitly rather than scanned-and-ignored — see the test file's own Scope comment for why a scan there would false-positive). A new file added to `commands/do-work/` is now covered automatically the moment it grows a real call site, with no array to remember to extend — closing the residual risk (a) actually had, at a fraction of the cost of (b).
- **(b) doesn't structurally eliminate the discipline problem — it relocates it.** The pitch for (b) is "collapse N re-derivation points to one choke point, correct by construction." But every `shipyard-config.sh get <path>` call site across the orchestrator would still need to know to read the snapshot instead of shelling out live — a call site that forgets and calls `shipyard-config.sh` directly reintroduces the identical bug class, just one level removed and over a much larger surface (every config read in the orchestrator, not only the subset that currently needs the `SHIPYARD_REPO_ROOT` pin). A regression test for that shape is also less mechanical to write than this one: "calls `shipyard-config.sh` but doesn't carry the pin substring" is a simple grep-shaped predicate; "calls `shipyard-config.sh` directly instead of reading the snapshot file" needs the same kind of predicate but now has to be applied to *every* config read in the tree, including many that have nothing to do with this bug.
- **(b) trades away mid-session config-mutation pickup, and `/shipyard:do-work` sessions are long-running.** A snapshot captured once at step 0.4 goes stale the moment anything changes the merged config mid-session — a maintainer editing `shipyard.config.json` or `.shipyard/config.local.json` while a many-hour autonomous session is in flight, or (per this repo's own `~97 PRs/week` cadence) a config-touching PR landing on `main` mid-session. Per-call-site re-derivation reads live every time, so it picks up any such change on the next read; a snapshot would silently keep serving the value captured at session start. #1059's own body already flagged this tradeoff; nothing in #1105's evidence changes the calculus in the snapshot's favor.
- **(b) is a materially larger, riskier change for a marginal gain now that the coverage gap has a mechanical fix.** #1059's own scope notes call out that (b) "changes the config-read contract everywhere" — every `shipyard-config.sh get <path>` invocation across the orchestrator, not just the ~10 `SHIPYARD_REPO_ROOT`-needing call sites, would need to change shape (either a direct rewrite to a `jq` lookup against a stashed snapshot file, or a `shipyard-config.sh --from-snapshot <path>` mode that keeps call sites textually similar but adds a second code path to `shipyard-config.sh` itself to maintain). That's a much bigger diff to review and a new failure class to reason about (snapshot invalidation, partial/corrupt snapshot writes, staleness) — for a gain that (a) + the mechanical-glob fix already captures for the coverage problem specifically, without touching the config-read contract at all.

**If this is ever revisited**, the trigger should be a new observed failure mode (a)'s live-read model can't address on its own — not a re-litigation of the residual-array-drift risk, which is now closed by the mechanical glob above. Should (b) ever become worth doing anyway (e.g. a future need to *snapshot* config for reproducibility, not just to avoid re-derivation), do not attempt it as a single PR: decompose into an ordered chain — (1) design the snapshot file's shape and the `shipyard-config.sh` read-mode it would need, as its own issue; (2) migrate call sites incrementally, file-by-file, each landing behind its own regression test; (3) only remove the `SHIPYARD_REPO_ROOT` pin machinery once every call site has migrated and the `shipyard-repo-root-preamble.test.sh` suite itself is retired as no-longer-applicable. Shipping the whole migration in one PR would touch too much of the orchestrator's spec tree at once to review safely, and would leave no safe rollback point if the snapshot shape needs revision after the first few call sites migrate.

## Mutually-blocking PR detection — why combining is out of scope (issue #1140)

**Decision: ship detection + loud surfacing only. Automated combining (cherry-picking the smaller PR's commit onto the larger) is deliberately NOT implemented.**

The repro: two open PRs against `mattsears18/lightwork` each fixed the exact required check the OTHER was failing (`🧹 Lint & Typecheck` and `🎭 Web E2E Tests`) — neither could go green alone, stalling an 11-PR-deep merge train, and the drain's only response was to apply `blocked:ci` to both with the real cause ("these two unblock each other") recorded nowhere. The issue's own body sketches a 4-step algorithm that ends in combining the PRs, and explicitly names the guardrails combining would need: file-disjoint diffs only, a hard cap on how many PRs may combine, and a loud comment on the PR. It also names the reason to be cautious: in the reporter's own manual repro, one of the two PRs was human-gated for an unrelated reason, and combining necessarily entangled them.

**Why detection-and-surfacing is the complete deliverable, not a partial one.** Before this change the state was not merely unresolved — it was *invisible*: `blocked:ci` looks identical whether a PR is stuck on a genuine unfixable failure or stuck because its sibling holds the fix. Making the relationship visible (a diagnosis comment naming the specific checks each PR blocks/is blocked by, whether the diffs are file-disjoint, and an end-of-session summary line) closes the actual reported gap — "the real explanation … nowhere in the record" — on its own, with zero risk of a bad automated mutation. A maintainer reading the diagnosis comment can combine manually in under a minute once they know the relationship exists; the hard part this issue reports is realizing the relationship exists at all.

**Why combining is a materially different risk class, not just "the same fix, automated":**

- **It mutates a PR branch that already has a life of its own** — commit history a reviewer has seen, possibly already-posted review comments, CI runs a human may be tracking. Rewriting one PR's branch to include the other's commit changes what "approve this PR" means after the fact, silently, unless the tooling is scrupulous about narrating it (which the issue itself flags as a requirement, not a nice-to-have).
- **The reporter's own repro entangled a PR that was human-gated for an unrelated reason.** A general-purpose automated combiner has no way to know a PR carries an out-of-band gate (a security review, a "don't merge yet" from a stakeholder) unless that gate is itself machine-readable — and most aren't. Detection-only never touches this landmine because it never touches a branch.
- **File-disjointness is necessary but not sufficient for a safe combine.** Two PRs can touch disjoint files and still be semantically entangled (one PR's disjoint-file change assumes an invariant the other PR's disjoint-file change breaks). The issue's own guardrail language ("only attempt when diffs are file-disjoint") is a floor, not a proof of safety — validating "file-disjoint" mechanically is cheap; validating "semantically safe to combine" is not, and a false green here ships a merge nobody actually reviewed as a unit.

**What was actually built.** `scripts/detect-mutually-blocking-prs.sh` is the single executable source of truth for the pairwise relationship (each PR's failing required checks fully covered by the other's passing checks — the "complete blocking set" the issue's step 2 describes), with a pure `--decide-pair` mode so the truth table is unit-tested without a live `gh`. It also reports file-disjointness as an informational signal (so the diagnosis comment can tell a human whether a manual combine is likely mechanical or needs real reconciliation), but disjointness never gates the detection verdict itself — the verdict is about the CI-blocking relationship, which holds regardless of whether combining would be easy. `drain.md`'s wiring is read-only against dispatch state: it changes nothing about `R_new` / fix-checks-only / `blocked:ci` routing, so a detected pair still settles through the exact same path it would have taken before this change — the only difference is that the settling now comes with a comment explaining why.

**Scope: pairs only, not N-PR cycles.** The issue's own phrasing is "a pair (or cycle)." A cycle spanning 3+ PRs is a real but rarer shape (it needs three simultaneous, mutually-covering breakages rather than two) and is left as a documented gap rather than guessed at — the reproduced failure, and the common real-world shape this issue names ("two repo-wide breakages landing within hours of each other"), is a pair.

**If automated combining is ever built**, it should be a separate, explicitly opt-in capability (a `ci.auto_combine_mutually_blocking: true` config knob, default off) — never a silent default — and should implement every guardrail the issue names as a precondition, not an afterthought: file-disjoint diffs only, a hard cap on PR count, and a loud, unambiguous comment on the surviving PR (and the one being superseded) narrating exactly what happened and why, posted *before* the combine takes effect where GitHub's UI allows it.

## Step D — recorded-probe recheck marker for external-dependency defers (issue #1198)

**Decision: ship the allowlist-only probe evaluator + read-side wiring into the proactive operator sweep now; leave scope-agent-automated marker authorship as a scoped follow-up rather than building it in this PR.**

[#1195](https://github.com/mattsears18/shipyard/issues/1195) closed the calendar-gate half of its own suggested direction: an `external-dependency` defer's re-litigation cost (one real issue re-diagnosed ~14 times over three weeks with an unchanged upstream fact each time) is capped by a self-clearing `<!-- do-work-blocked-until: YYYY-MM-DD -->` body marker the proactive sweep honors. That closed PR's own body explicitly deferred the higher-value, higher-risk half — a **recorded probe**: a marker naming a command that mechanically tests the real condition, turning "re-investigate from scratch" into "run one command and compare" — and filed it as this issue, because executing a command sourced from issue-body text is a genuine code-execution surface on a repo whose entire worker-preamble discipline treats issue-body content as untrusted regardless of author trust.

**Why allowlist-only, never sanitized free text.** The issue's own suggested direction named this explicitly ("reject anything that doesn't match a known-safe verb rather than attempting to sanitize free-form shell text"), and `scripts/eval-recheck-probe.sh`'s design follows it to the letter: exactly two verbs were recognized (`npm-view`, `gh-api`) — three as of [#1496](https://github.com/mattsears18/shipyard/issues/1496), which added `url-json` under the same discipline — each field is validated against an anchored character-class regex before use, and every value reaches its target binary as its own bash-array element — never through `eval`, never through a `sh -c` string, never concatenated. A regex gap in this design still could not become a second command, because there is no shell re-parse step downstream of validation for it to escape into; the allowlist regexes are a semantic narrowing (which endpoints/fields are *meaningful* to probe) layered on top of that structural guarantee, not the only thing standing between untrusted text and code execution.

**Why the gh-api allowlist is narrower than "any GET is safe."** A GET request can't mutate anything, but it can still read data the marker author (untrusted, potentially edited after the fact) shouldn't get to name arbitrarily — collaborator lists, webhook configs, Actions secret *names* (not values, but still information disclosure), deploy keys. The evaluator's endpoint grammar allowlists five specific sub-resource shapes (`releases/latest`, `tags`, `commits/<sha>`, `issues/<n>`, `pulls/<n>`) — the ones a "has upstream shipped/closed/merged the fix" probe actually needs — and explicitly excludes every sensitive sub-resource by omission, plus locks the `<owner>/<repo>` segment to the repo the evaluation was invoked for (no cross-repo probing at all, even of another public repo the same `gh` token can read). This is deliberately narrower than what the issue's own suggested direction sketched ("`gh api` read-only calls," unqualified) — per this dispatch's guardrails, a narrower allowlist than the issue imagines is the correct default; widen it only via a deliberate, reviewed change to the allowlist regex, never by loosening validation to "whatever the marker says."

**Why every failure mode degrades to `unknown`, and why `unknown` is treated identically to `absent`/`unchanged`.** A probe can fail for reasons that have nothing to do with the blocking condition — a network blip, an npm registry format change, a typo'd field name, the timeout firing. If any of those degraded toward "changed" instead of "unknown," an inconclusive or erroring read would bypass the calendar gate exactly the way a genuinely-resolved condition should — the one thing this feature must never do, since the marker text driving the probe is untrusted. The evaluator's own comparison function (`decide()`) treats an empty actual value as `unknown` before it ever compares against `expected`, and every caller-side branch (malformed marker, unrecognized verb, nonzero exit, timeout, empty output) routes to the same `unknown` verdict with the same "fall through to the calendar-only check" instruction. This mirrors `detect-ungated-admin-direct-merge.sh`'s stated fail-safe posture: any signal that cannot be obtained resolves toward the slower-but-safe path, never toward the faster-but-riskier one.

**Why authorship is human-only in this slice, and what's deliberately NOT built yet.** The issue's own body poses "who can author a probe command" as an open question, flagging that even a marker written by a trusted-author's scope agent could later be edited by a third party's comment before the orchestrator re-reads it — and, separately, that a scope agent deriving a probe from an issue's `evidence_pointer` is itself deriving structured content from what is fundamentally untrusted body text. Wiring automated authorship into the `external-dependency` class's own body-marker write path additionally depends on that write path existing — it's [#1199](https://github.com/mattsears18/shipyard/issues/1199)'s step 4b, not yet merged at the time this issue was worked. Rather than block this PR on #1199 merging first, or duplicate its not-yet-reviewed step 4b insertion point (real conflict risk for zero benefit — both PRs would be constructing the same insertion point independently), this slice ships the evaluator and the READ side only, wired generically against ANY class that writes a `do-work-blocked-until` marker (today: `time-gated`; `external-dependency` inherits the capability automatically the moment #1199 lands, with zero further code changes, because both classes share the identical marker format). Authorship starts at the safest possible principal — a human hand-writing the marker directly into the issue body, the same trust level already established for `do-work-blocked-until` itself (issue #1161's precedent: a human can hand-write that marker and the step-4 filter honors it with no scope-agent involvement required). Automated, scope-agent-derived probe authorship — where the real "structured content synthesized from untrusted text" risk lives, and where per-class write-time validation deserves its own dedicated review once #1199's `external-dependency` step 4b is on `main` to extend — is filed as a follow-up rather than attempted here.

**Why the read-side wiring lives in the proactive sweep and not the step-4 backlog filter.** `time-gated` issues carry no label — the `blocked-until` marker IS their entire dispatch gate, read by `04-backlog-divert.md`'s step-4 client-side filter. `external-dependency` issues (and, once #1199 lands, their `blocked-until` marker) are already excluded from `raw_backlog` structurally by the `agent-console` label; the marker's payoff there is purely about the proactive sweep's re-enqueue cadence, per #1195. Since this issue is scoped to the `external-dependency` re-litigation-cost problem specifically, the probe-eval wiring targets the same call site #1195 already modified (the sweep), not the backlog filter — extending the latter to also read a probe marker for `time-gated` issues is a plausible future enhancement but out of scope here, and left undone rather than added speculatively.

## Step 4 — generalizing the recheck probe into the backlog filter itself, and splitting the deferral taxonomy into time gates vs. event gates (issue #1356)

**Decision: extend the probe evaluator into `scripts/backlog-filter.sh classify` (the step-4 dispatch filter's own executable definition), gated by presence of a companion `do-work-recheck` marker rather than by `defer_reason_class` — never introduce a third marker name.** This is exactly the "plausible future enhancement" #1198's own RATIONALE section (immediately above) predicted and left undone. The trigger: a real lightwork session found that of 11 open issues carrying `do-work-blocked-until`, two were genuinely EVENT-gated ("once real users leave written store reviews," "once an Expo SDK bump moves metro") wearing an invented placeholder date — and because the step-4 filter only ever compared the calendar date, once that date lapsed the issue silently re-entered `raw_backlog`, got re-diagnosed from scratch, and re-deferred with a freshly invented date. Repeat forever: a churn loop that burns tokens every session and reports a stable-looking backlog even though nothing actually moved.

**Why a second marker name (`do-work-blocked-on-probe`) was considered and rejected.** The issue's own suggested fix sketched a brand-new marker form carrying an executable predicate. But `<!-- do-work-recheck: <verb> <args...> == <expected> -->` already IS exactly that — a marker naming a mechanically-checkable command with an expected-value contract — built, allowlisted, and tested by #1198. Inventing a second marker with the identical shape and a different name would fork the evaluator's own read logic (which name means what) with zero behavioral gain over reusing the existing one. The marker-syntax distinction the acceptance criteria ask for ("time gates and event gates are distinguishable by marker syntax") is satisfied by **pairing**, not by a new name: `do-work-blocked-until` ALONE is a time gate (the date is the constraint, unchanged from #1161/#1195); `do-work-blocked-until` PAIRED WITH `do-work-recheck` is an event gate (the probe verdict is the constraint, the date becomes a documentation-only "roughly when we expect to check again" note). This is also why `time_gate_future`'s existing line-1 "position discipline" ([#1168](https://github.com/mattsears18/shipyard/issues/1168)) was deliberately left untouched rather than relaxed to scan the whole body — relaxing it would have let a `do-work-recheck` marker accidentally written above the calendar marker silently break the calendar gate for every plain time-gated issue too (see the `06d` ordering-bug fix below, which is the write-side half of the exact same hazard).

**Why `classify` stays a pure function — the live probe evaluation is a new, separate subcommand (`eval-probes`), not inlined into `classify` itself.** `classify`'s own design note (top of `backlog-filter.sh`) states the reason this split already exists for `closed-by-healthy-pr`: the classification DECISION is what has actually drifted historically (#332, #1194) and is what needs to stay fixture-testable against fixed inputs with zero network calls, forever. `eval-probes` performs the live `npm view`/`gh api` calls (via `eval-recheck-probe.sh --bulk`, itself a thin iteration wrapper — no second copy of the marker grammar or allowlist) and hands `classify` a precomputed `{number: verdict}` map via `--probe-verdicts`. `classify`'s own event-gate branch is pure lookup-and-branch: no entry in the map, or an explicit `unchanged`/`unknown`, both fail safe to `event-gated`; only an explicit `changed` admits. This mirrors `eval-recheck-probe.sh`'s own never-treat-inconclusive-as-resolved posture at one more layer up the call stack.

**Why the class-level kill switch (`--recheck-probe-enabled`, mirroring `scope.recheck_probe_enabled`) is a `classify` flag rather than something `classify` reads from config itself.** Keeping `classify` free of ANY I/O — not just network I/O, but also a `shipyard-config.sh get` shell-out — is what makes it safely fixture-testable with a bare `jq` pipeline and no environment setup. The caller (`04-backlog-divert.md` step 4, `steady-state.md` step C, `drain.md`'s termination assertion) reads the config knob once and passes the resolved boolean through, the same pattern `--respect-assignees` and the two `--milestones-*` flags already established.

**Why `06d-recheck-probe-authorship.md`'s automated-write trigger widened from `external-dependency` only to `external-dependency` OR `time-gated`, rather than staying scoped.** The acceptance criteria are explicit: "any defer class can carry a probe, not only `external-dependency`." Auditing the two classes that actually write a `do-work-blocked-until` marker at all (the only two eligible, since a probe pairs with a calendar marker that must already exist), `time-gated`'s own original exclusion rationale — "it has no upstream fact to probe, only a date" — turns out to be a definitional assumption, not an enforced one: nothing stopped a scope agent (or a human) from picking `time-gated` for a genuinely event-shaped block precisely because forecasting a date is what the class asks for. Widening the trigger lets that same `evidence_pointer` hint land a probe regardless of which of the two classes produced it, closing the gap rather than requiring every event-gated defer to be reclassified as `external-dependency` first.

**The `06d` write-ordering bug this issue also fixed, found while implementing the above.** `06d`'s original write step unconditionally prepended `$MARKER_LINE\n\n$CURRENT_BODY` — since it always runs immediately after the calendar marker write (step 4a/4b), `$CURRENT_BODY`'s own first line was always the just-written `do-work-blocked-until` marker, and the unconditional prepend silently pushed it to a later line, breaking `time_gate_future`'s line-1 position-discipline check for every issue that ever exercised this path (a marker no longer on line 1 reads as absent, so the issue would have become immediately dispatchable regardless of its date). This was a latent bug in the original #1201 slice — it never regressed in production only because the recognized-hint precondition (`evidence_pointer` carrying a valid `Recheck probe:` clause) was rare enough that the path had likely never fired for real. Fixed by inserting the recheck marker as line 2 (immediately after the calendar marker) instead of prepending above it, plus a belt-and-suspenders read-back check confirming the calendar marker is still on line 1 after the write.

**No-cheap-probe case (acceptance criterion 5).** `eval-recheck-probe.sh`'s allowlist is deliberately narrow (`npm-view`, `gh-api`, and — per [#1496](https://github.com/mattsears18/shipyard/issues/1496) — `url-json`, itself inert until the repo allowlists a host; all per #1198's own threat-model rationale above) — most genuinely event-gated conditions have no single-command mechanical test. `06-scope-preflight.md`'s `time-gated` class entry now says plainly what to do instead: write `do-work-blocked-until` alone with a deliberately distant date (a conscious park, not a plausible-looking near-term guess) rather than inventing a rolling near-term date every re-diagnosis. This doesn't eliminate the churn loop for the no-probe case — it can't, since there is no mechanical signal to consult — but it makes each re-litigation honest about being a park rather than manufacturing the illusion of near-term progress.

## Step 4 — splitting "is this PR healthy?" from "is this issue covered?" (issue #1389)

**Decision: add a `closed-by-open-pr` SIBLING subcommand + a `--closed-by-open-pr` classify flag, and leave `closed-by-healthy-pr` byte-for-byte unchanged.** The pre-#1389 filter dropped an issue from `raw_backlog` only when its open `@me` PR was *healthy*. That "healthy" qualifier is correct for the question it was originally written to answer — *should this PR go in `failed_prs`?* — and it must keep answering exactly that. The defect was reuse: the same computation was being consulted for a second, different question — *should this ISSUE go in `raw_backlog`?* — where check health is simply not a relevant input. An issue whose open `@me` PR names it in `closingIssuesReferences` is **covered** whether that PR is green, red, or `DIRTY`; a red PR is fix-checks work *on the PR* and a `DIRTY` one is fix-rebase work *on the PR*, so an issue-worker dispatched at either can only reach `issue-work.md` step 0's duplicate-PR bail. The measured cost in the reported session (`do-work-20260815T014844Z-40700` against `mattsears18/lightwork`): one open, `BLOCKED`, mid-CI PR closed five issues; three of them were scoped READY and dispatched anyway — 3 worker + 3 scope-agent dispatches, ~250k tokens — and the PR merged 35 minutes later, closing all five.

**Why a sibling rather than widening the existing clause.** Widening `closed-by-healthy-pr` would have silently changed the meaning of a name three call-sites and a fixture suite already depend on, and would have destroyed the `failed_prs` routing signal that health test exists to produce. Keeping two narrow computations with two accurate names is what makes the two questions stay distinguishable — the exact property whose absence caused this bug.

**Why the covered clause is evaluated AFTER the healthy clause.** The covered set is a strict *superset* of the healthy set, so evaluating it first would relabel every already-dropping healthy row from `closed-by-healthy-pr` to `covered-by-open-pr` — a gratuitous output-contract change for rows whose behavior isn't changing. Ordering it second means only the two genuinely-new rows (open+red, open+`DIRTY`) ever emit the new reason, and every pre-#1389 verdict string is reproduced verbatim.

**Why the drop carries an `evidence_pointer` naming the covering PR.** [`04f-completion-ledger.md`](./do-work/setup/04f-completion-ledger.md) has to distinguish *covered* (work in flight on a PR the orchestrator is already tracking — bucket 3) from *dropped* (filtered out of the queue). A bare `drop` verdict can't carry that distinction, and re-deriving the covering PR at ledger-render time would mean a second `gh` walk. Carrying `PR #<M> closingIssuesReferences includes #<N>` in the verdict makes the ledger line renderable with zero additional calls — the same `evidence_pointer` convention `tracking` already established (#1364).

**On the "no new network query" constraint.** The issue notes the data is already fetched and asks for a filter change rather than a second query. Strictly, this ships a second `gh pr list` — but a *cheaper* one: `closed-by-open-pr`'s projection is `number,closingIssuesReferences` only, with no `statusCheckRollup` (the expensive per-check array `closed-by-healthy-pr` needs). The alternative — one combined subcommand emitting both sets — would have required restructuring all three `classify` call-sites and stripping `closed-by-healthy-pr` of the consumers the issue explicitly says it should keep, to save one cheap REST call per setup pass. Not worth it.

**#332 is preserved by construction, not by convention.** The `closed/abandoned PR → issue stays dispatchable` row is protected because `closed-by-open-pr` queries `--state open` only: a closed or merged-without-closing PR is never returned, so its issue never enters the map. That's a structural property of the query, not a rule someone has to remember — and it's pinned by its own assertion in the fixture suite alongside the two newly-dropped rows.

## `milestones` config block — config-surface-only scope, and why `projects` (Projects v2) was dropped (issue #1239)

**Decision: ship the schema block + accessor defaults + `/shipyard:init` offering only. No consuming behavior (dispatch ranking, filing-time assignment, the loop-end sweep) is implemented in this issue.**

#1239 is the first of a five-issue milestones/roadmap sequence (#1239–#1244) the maintainer filed with the design decisions already settled, not open for reinterpretation by the implementer. The four settled decisions, and why each one is fixed rather than a knob:

- **`milestones` is top-level and independent of everything else — no `projects` (GitHub Projects v2) block.** A Projects v2 board was designed alongside this and explicitly dropped: the board carried four fields, three of which (`Status`, `Priority`, `Horizon`) were derived restatements of issue state and labels shipyard already tracks natively — the two-sources-that-drift failure a config surface should never introduce on purpose. Projects v2 also requires the `project` OAuth scope, which `gh` does not grant by default, so wiring it in would add real onboarding friction for a feature that mostly restates existing signal. Keeping `milestones` and any future `projects` block independent (rather than nesting one under the other, or sharing a parent object) is what makes adding `projects` later — if it's ever warranted — cheap and non-breaking.
- **Phase authoring is the agent's job, not the config's — no declarative `phases[]` array.** Shipyard *names* phases and writes each one's bet/done-criteria from the repo's actual backlog at the point a phase is warranted; there is nothing for a maintainer to hand-author in `shipyard.config.json` up front. This is a deliberate divergence from the lightwork skill this issue ports from, which required proposing every new phase — see the maintainer's milestone-creation-authority split below.
- **Ordering is a numeric title prefix `N · Title`** (U+00B7 MIDDLE DOT, spaced on both sides), not a config field. The separator is fixed, not configurable: a configurable separator is a parsing surface with no offsetting benefit, since the format only ever needs to round-trip through shipyard's own read/write path, never through a human editing raw config. Renumbering later phases to insert one mid-sequence is safe because issues reference milestones by GitHub's own numeric milestone ID, not by title — a rename never breaks an assignment.
- **The fallback milestone never completes and always sorts last**, default title `Ongoing maintenance` (overridable via `milestones.fallback`), created automatically the first time `enabled` flips true. It exists so an issue that fits no phase never sits unmilestoned while a human decides where it belongs.

**Why config-surface-only, not a thin vertical slice through to dispatch.** Each of #1240–#1244 reads exactly one key this schema defines (`prioritize_dispatch`, `assign_on_file`, `sweep_on_loop_end`), so the feature degrades cleanly — a repo can take milestone-ordered dispatch without filing-time assignment, or vice versa, once those land independently. Building the schema and the first consumer together in one PR would couple this foundation issue's landing to a single downstream feature's design, when the whole point of splitting into five issues was to let each consumer land (and be reviewed) on its own. The acceptance criteria are correspondingly narrow and mechanical: schema validates the block, accessors return documented defaults when the block is absent, a repo with no block behaves byte-for-byte as it does today (see the [`dont.md` inertness bullet](./do-work/dont.md) this issue adds — every future consumer must gate on `milestones.enabled` before doing anything observable), and `/shipyard:init` offers it.

**Maintainer decision — milestone-creation authority, split three ways (recorded on the issue after initial filing).** This supersedes the issue body's earlier, broader claim that shipyard has free rein to create milestones:

| Case | Authority | Why |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Cold start — repo has zero phases | Apply | Proposing would leave the entire backlog unmilestoned pending a human; nothing at risk since there's no existing roadmap to disturb. |
| The fallback milestone | Apply | Plumbing, not strategy — never completes, always sorts last. |
| A new phase in an established roadmap | Propose | Claims a slot in the sequence, renumbers existing phases, asserts a new bet — a strategy change. |

An issue that fits no existing phase goes to the fallback **immediately** while a phase proposal is pending, rather than sitting in a "no milestone" limbo — otherwise proposing would violate the every-open-issue-carries-a-milestone invariant the moment the sweep found a gap. This restores the boundary the ported lightwork skill originally drew and that the issue's initial "free rein" framing had loosened: fill a gap mechanically, never re-sequence a human-authored plan without asking.

## `shipyard:update-roadmap` skill — why the worker prohibition is a separate fragment, why dependency detection splits apply/propose the way it does, and the source-unreachable fallback (issue #1240)

**Decision: port lightwork's `.claude/skills/update-roadmap/SKILL.md` as `plugins/shipyard/skills/update-roadmap/SKILL.md`, generalized off the literal repo/config values, with the GitHub Projects v2 half dropped and dependency detection added as new scope.** This is the first *consumer* of the `milestones` config block #1239 shipped as scaffolding — it reads `milestones.enabled` and `milestones.fallback` only; the other three keys (`assign_on_file`, `prioritize_dispatch`, `sweep_on_loop_end`) belong to #1241–#1243 and are deliberately untouched here.

**Why the worker prohibition lives in a `shipyard:worker-preamble` fragment, not only in the skill file.** The issue itself called this out explicitly: a rule stated only in `shipyard:update-roadmap` (the skill the *orchestrator* runs) is invisible to the agent it's meant to constrain — a dispatched worker never loads that skill in the ordinary course of its own dispatch. `shipyard:worker-preamble` already carries 18 prohibitions as dedicated fragment files for exactly this reason (`git-stash-prohibition.md`, `compound-command-refusal.md`, `nvm-source-refusal.md`, and so on) rather than leaving each one stated once wherever the feature happens to be documented — a prohibition is only load-bearing if it lives where the constrained agent actually reads it. `milestone-prohibition.md` follows that precedent: it states the same rule, but from the worker's own vantage point, with the one carve-out (a worker filing a follow-up issue may stamp it with the *parent's own existing* milestone — inherited filing metadata, not a new milestone decision) spelled out where a worker would actually need it.

**Why dependency detection splits `Blocked by #N` into apply-if-stated / propose-if-inferred**, mirroring the skill's existing autonomy spine rather than introducing a new rule shape: a stated dependency ("once #N lands", "after the config block ships") is a fact the issue's own author already committed to text — recording it is transcription, not judgment. An *inferred* dependency — noticed only by reading two issues together, neither of which states it — carries real risk if wrong: `Blocked by #N` is a **hard gate** ([`01b-backlog-overview.md` bucket 7](./do-work/setup/01b-backlog-overview.md)) that removes an issue from `/do-work`'s workable queue entirely, so a wrong inferred block silently stalls real work with no visible symptom other than "why hasn't this issue been picked up." Applying only the stated case and proposing the inferred case keeps the failure mode of a wrong dependency call to "a human has to reject a proposal," not "an issue silently never dispatches."

**Why file-level conflict inference between issues is explicitly out of scope.** `detect-mutually-blocking-prs.sh` already handles the PR-side collision case (two open PRs touching the same file/section); extending dependency detection to guess file-level conflicts from *issue* text alone — before any code exists — would produce false blocks in exactly the place a false positive is most expensive: an issue that's actually fine to dispatch, silently removed from the queue on a guess. The skill states this exclusion directly rather than leaving it to be rediscovered by a future maintainer wondering why dependency detection doesn't also catch file collisions.

**Source-unreachable fallback, and why proceeding was the right call rather than blocking.** At implementation time, `mattsears18/lightwork`'s `.claude/skills/update-roadmap/SKILL.md` returned 404 against that repo's `main` branch via the GitHub Contents API, and a full-tree search (`git/trees/main?recursive=1`, filtered on `roadmap`) found no matching path anywhere in that branch's reachable history — the `.claude/skills/` directory in that repo has no `update-roadmap` subdirectory at all. The issue body anticipated exactly this possibility and pre-authorized proceeding from the issue's own text rather than blocking: the autonomy boundary table, the honesty checks, the anti-WIP-limit rule, the cold-start handling, the milestone-creation-authority split, and the "prove a check can fail" cautionary note were all specified in the issue body in enough detail to reconstruct the original's substance. Blocking on an unreachable private-repo path that the issue explicitly said not to block on would have been the wrong failure mode — the issue's own fallback instruction is dispositive here, not merely a suggestion to weigh against "try harder to find the file."

## Milestone-aware backlog ranking — `backlog-filter.sh classify`'s `_sort_key`, and why the tiers sit where they do (issue #1241)

`/do-work` ranked its backlog by priority alone — `P0` > `P1` > `P2` > unlabeled, then staleness — with no notion of *sequence*. A repo that had sequenced its work into milestones (via `milestones.enabled` from [#1239](#milestones-config-block--config-surface-only-scope-and-why-projects-projects-v2-was-dropped-issue-1239)) got no benefit from having done so: the dispatcher would pick the highest-priority issue anywhere, and a `P1` in the last phase would outrank a `P2` in the phase actually being worked. `milestones.prioritize_dispatch` (also shipped inert by #1239) is the flag this issue wires up.

**Where the ranking actually lives, and why this issue is a script change, not a prose one.** The issue body was filed describing the ranking as living in `04-backlog-divert.md`'s prose. By the time this issue was worked, [#1247](https://github.com/mattsears18/shipyard/issues/1247) had already extracted the whole eligibility-and-ranking predicate into `scripts/backlog-filter.sh`'s `classify` subcommand, marking every prose description across the three call-sites (`setup/04-backlog-divert.md`, `steady-state.md` step C, `drain.md`'s termination-assertion) explicitly non-normative. So the milestone-aware tier lives in the script's `_sort_key` computation, not as a fourth independently-authored prose copy — the exact drift #1247 exists to prevent.

**The maintainer's final tier ordering, reviewed and recorded on the issue, superseding two earlier draft orderings in the issue body:**

```
0. Hard gates (drop from the queue, never sort): open Blocked by #N,
   needs-human-review, assigned, blocked:*
1. P0 — global, any milestone
2. --prioritize-label — explicit per-run operator override
3. Milestone — earliest phase with dispatchable work
4. P1 > P2 > unlabeled  ┐
5. Type                 ├─ tiebreakers, NOT priorities
6. Staleness            ┘
```

**The three tiers are different in kind, and conflating them is how this gets mis-implemented.** `P0` is an *emergency escape* — it exists precisely to violate the plan, so it sits at tier 1, global, outranking even milestone sequence. The milestone tier (3) *is* the plan — the whole point of this issue. Tiers 4–6 are pure tiebreaking, not priorities at all — under parallel dispatch, within-milestone order barely changes outcomes (nearly every issue in a phase gets worked in the same session regardless of who goes first), so they are deliberately left low-stakes: don't over-invest tuning them, and don't add more tiers (no issue-number tiebreaker was imported from lightwork's own ordering — shipyard already had `type` as an innermost tiebreaker lightwork's list didn't have, and mixing the two systems would silently replace staleness for milestone-enabled repos while claiming to only add milestone awareness).

**Change to existing behavior: `P0` now outranks `--prioritize-label` — but ONLY when milestone ranking is actually on.** Before this issue, the prioritized-label tier was outermost in `classify`'s sort key (`sort_by([._prioritized_tier, ._priority_rank, ...])`), so a `--prioritize-label`-matching non-P0 issue could rank above a P0. The maintainer's read: that's backwards — the flag reorders the *normal backlog*, and a `P0` is by definition not normal backlog; an operator who passed `--prioritize-label docs` without knowing a production incident was open would want the incident first. This reordering (P0 promoted above `_prioritized_tier`) is real and intentional, but it is gated behind `milestone_rank_on` (`milestones.enabled && milestones.prioritize_dispatch`, both true) rather than applied unconditionally — the issue's own acceptance criteria required "`milestones.enabled: false` or `prioritize_dispatch: false` → ranking byte-identical to today," and an unconditional P0-above-prioritize-label swap would have broken that guarantee for every repo that has never touched the `milestones` block at all. So the "change to existing behavior" language in the issue body reads as a general statement but is implemented as a milestone-scoped one — the two are reconciled by making the promotion an *effect of opting into milestone ranking*, not a standalone behavior change shipped to every repo.

**Two branches, not one formula, is what makes byte-identical provable rather than merely likely.** `CLASSIFY_JQ`'s `_sort_key` computes one of two structurally different arrays depending on `$milestone_rank_on`: the off-branch is a verbatim copy of the pre-#1241 four-element key (`[_prioritized_tier, _priority_rank, _type_rank, _updatedAt]`); the on-branch is a six-element key with the P0 escape and milestone sequence inserted. An earlier design considered a single unified formula (e.g., always compute a P0-escape rank but multiply it by a milestone-on/off toggle) — rejected because a shared formula creates exactly the kind of subtle cross-contamination a "prove byte-identical" acceptance criterion is supposed to rule out categorically, not empirically. Two independent branches means the off-branch literally cannot be affected by anything the on-branch does, by construction, not by testing.

**"Earliest milestone with dispatchable work" needs no separate computation — it falls out of sorting eligible issues alone.** A phase with zero *eligible* issues (everything in it hard-gated, assigned, or dropped) simply never appears in the eligible bucket at all, so sorting the eligible bucket ascending by milestone sequence number automatically skips it — the third acceptance criterion ("a phase whose only remaining work is `needs-human-review` does not stall the loop") is a structural consequence of the classifier already having dropped/gated/routed away everything that isn't eligible *before* the milestone sort ever runs, not a separate check this issue had to add. This is also why milestone ranking scopes to `raw_backlog` only, never `investigate_candidates` — the investigate list already has its own narrower rank order (priority + staleness, no type or prioritized-label tier either), and mixing milestone sequencing into a queue whose entire purpose is "surface untriaged/bot-authored work regardless of the roadmap" would work against the queue's own point.

**Unmilestoned and unparseable-title issues sort to the tail, never drop.** `milestone_seq($issue)` returns a sentinel (`999999999`) for both an issue with no `milestone` field at all and one whose milestone title doesn't match the `N · Title` prefix. As originally shipped this was "sorts with the fallback" read loosely rather than literally — the reasoning recorded here was that determining the *real* fallback milestone's sequence number would require a second, non-pure network call the script's design explicitly avoids, and that only "doesn't get silently dropped from the ranked list" was load-bearing. **[#1499](#unmilestoned-ties-with-the-fallback-milestone-rather-than-losing-to-it-issue-1499) superseded the loose reading**: the fallback's `N` turns out to be readable straight out of the wide-fetch payload `classify` already receives, so the literal tie costs no network call and no loss of purity. See that section for why the loose reading was not merely imprecise but an active severity inversion.

**Three consequences the issue explicitly pre-declared correct, not defects — recorded here so a future session doesn't "fix" them:**

1. **Late-phase work can sit indefinitely.** That's sequencing working as designed. No starvation valve (e.g., "every Nth dispatch takes the oldest issue regardless of phase") was added — one would quietly undo the ordering this whole issue exists to introduce.
2. **The fallback milestone is only reached when real phases have nothing dispatchable.** Not a bug — the never-block-on-a-human-gated-phase rule (hard gate 0, applied before the milestone sort ever sees the issue) is what feeds it, so routine fallback/maintenance work runs exactly when the real phases are stalled on a human, which is the right time for it to run.
3. **A mis-milestoned issue becomes near-invisible.** This IS a genuinely new failure mode relative to flat-priority ranking — under flat priority a mislabeled `P2` still surfaces eventually as staleness accumulates; under strict phase order, an issue filed into the wrong (especially a too-late) phase may sit for a long time. `shipyard:update-roadmap`'s phase-order honesty check ([#1240](#shipyardupdate-roadmap-skill--why-the-worker-prohibition-is-a-separate-fragment-why-dependency-detection-splits-applypropose-the-way-it-does-and-the-source-unreachable-fallback-issue-1240)'s "is phase order being respected" check) is the only guard against this, which is why that check's importance is called out explicitly rather than left as one bullet among several.

**Test coverage lives in a dedicated suite, not folded into `backlog-filter.test.sh`.** `scripts/tests/backlog-milestone-ranking.test.sh` is a new file rather than new assertions appended to the existing `backlog-filter.test.sh` — the existing suite already covers `classify`'s eligibility predicate exhaustively (52 assertions) and reads as a complete, closed suite for that concern; milestone ranking is a large enough, separable enough feature (covering both AND-gate legs independently, the P0-global escape, the prioritize-label interaction, the tail sentinel for both absent and malformed milestone titles, within-milestone tiebreakers, investigate-list non-interference, and — since #1499 — the unmilestoned/fallback tie) to warrant its own file, matching the repo's per-feature test-file convention (`audit-schedule.test.sh`, `decompose-epic.test.sh`, `milestones-config.test.sh`, and so on) rather than growing one script's suite indefinitely as every future feature touching that script lands.

## Unmilestoned ties with the fallback milestone rather than losing to it (issue #1499)

**Decision: remove the inversion at the source — remap `milestone_seq`'s sentinel onto the fallback milestone's own sequence number — rather than keep the ordering and emit a classify-time advisory about it.** The issue offered both; the alternative (surface it) preserves a silent severity override and adds a signal some downstream caller then has to notice and act on, which is strictly more machinery for a strictly worse outcome. Fixing the order also makes the code match the intent `milestone_seq`'s own comment had been claiming all along.

**The bug, precisely.** `milestone_seq` returns `999999999` for an unmilestoned (or unparseable-milestone) issue, and `_sort_key` puts the milestone tier **above** `priority_rank`. So an unmilestoned issue lost to *every* milestoned issue — including one in the fallback phase, which is the catch-all for work that fits no phase, i.e. semantically the identical state. Because milestone is the more significant key, that loss was not a tiebreak but a wholesale override of severity: a fallback-milestoned `P2`, and even a fallback-milestoned issue with no priority label at all, ranked ahead of three unmilestoned `P1`s in the session that filed this issue. `assign_on_file` ([#1242](#milestone-assignment-at-filing-time--matching-against-bet-why-a-filer-never-creates-a-phase-and-sub-issue-inheritance-issue-1242)) is what makes unmilestoned issues rare, but any hole in it converted directly into a silent severity inversion, and `sweep_on_loop_end` ([#1243](#end-of-loop-milestone-roadmap-sweep--why-its-non-fatal-why-it-skips-on-a-no-op-loop-and-why-the-skills-own-step-7-report-is-suppressed-here-issue-1243)) only backfills at the *end* of a session — after that session already dispatched in the wrong order.

**Why the fallback's `N` is read back from the input, not taken as a config number.** #1241 declined the literal tie on the grounds that it would need a network call and cost `classify` its purity. That premise doesn't hold: the wide-fetch payload `classify` already reads carries each issue's milestone *title*, so the caller only has to supply the fallback's **bare title** (the `milestones.fallback` config value — matched via the existing `milestone_title_bare()` normalization, exactly as `--someday-milestone` is) and `fallback_milestone_seq()` recovers the number from whichever input issues carry that milestone. No I/O, no second flag to keep in sync with the repo's actual milestone list, and no number a maintainer could get wrong by hand.

**Why not derive the fallback from "the highest `N` present in the input" and skip the flag entirely.** Tempting — the schema does guarantee the fallback holds the highest `N` — but it only holds when the fallback *has open issues*. When it doesn't, the highest `N` present belongs to some real earlier phase, and unmilestoned issues would be silently promoted into that phase's tier: a new inversion in the opposite direction, in a case the issue never asked about. Naming the milestone is what keeps the change scoped to exactly the equivalence it claims.

**Why the flag defaults to off rather than to the schema's own `"Ongoing maintenance"`.** `classify` is a pure function whose defaults exist to protect callers and fixtures that predate a flag; baking a config default into the script would make the script's behavior diverge from what its own arguments say. `classify-backlog.sh` — the one caller that *does* read config — resolves `milestones.fallback` and passes it, so every real `/do-work` session gets the tie without any orchestrator-side prose change. With the flag absent, ranking is byte-identical to pre-#1499, which is what the existing pinned fixtures assert.

**Two invariants the implementation preserves by construction, both pinned by fixtures.** The milestone-**off** branch of `_sort_key` is untouched — `milestone_rank` appears only in the on-branch, so the "`milestones.enabled:false` OR `prioritize_dispatch:false` ⇒ byte-identical ranking" guarantee #1241 built two separate branches to make provable still holds structurally, not empirically. And the tie is scoped to the fallback phase alone: an unmilestoned issue is never promoted past a genuine earlier phase, whatever its priority label.

**Why `$unmilestoned_seq` is hoisted out of the per-issue map.** It is a property of the *backlog*, not of any issue, so it is computed once at the top of the pipeline and threaded into `classify_one` as a parameter. Computing it from the *eligible* bucket instead would silently reinstate the inversion whenever the repo's only fallback-milestoned issues happen to be gated or dropped — case (23) in `backlog-milestone-ranking.test.sh` pins that.

## Milestone assignment at filing time — matching against `BET:`, why a filer never creates a phase, and sub-issue inheritance (issue #1242)

**Decision: wire `milestones.assign_on_file` into the two shared filing surfaces (`shipyard:filing-github-issues`, `/shipyard:file-issue`) and into `/decompose-epic`'s sub-issue creation, as documentation-level guidance for the filing agent rather than a deterministic script.** Every one of these three call sites is an LLM agent reading and following markdown instructions when it files a `gh issue create` — there is no shipyard-owned shell script that decides *which* milestone a given finding belongs to, so "implementation" here means writing the matching rule precisely enough that the agent's own judgment reproduces the same placement `shipyard:update-roadmap`'s unmilestoned sweep would make.

**Why match against each milestone's `BET:`, never against title keywords.** The issue's own body cites a concrete incident: `shipyard:update-roadmap`'s "Every open issue gets a milestone" section already warns that a blanket "everything unmilestoned → maintenance" sweep once swallowed two `P0` defects that were breaking `main` and a Cloud Functions deploy, because the sweep matched on superficial title signal ("fix", "bug") rather than reading what the phase's bet actually claims to be doing. Filing-time assignment is the same match performed earlier in the issue's lifecycle and by a different caller (an auditor or a human, not the roadmap sweep) — reusing the sweep's own matching discipline keeps the two callers from disagreeing about where an issue belongs, and avoids reintroducing the exact failure mode the sweep already learned to avoid.

**Why a filer must never create a milestone, not even the fallback.** `shipyard:update-roadmap`'s milestone-creation-authority table (see the [`shipyard:update-roadmap` skill entry](#shipyardupdate-roadmap-skill--why-the-worker-prohibition-is-a-separate-fragment-why-dependency-detection-splits-applypropose-the-way-it-does-and-the-source-unreachable-fallback-issue-1240) above) already draws this line for the *sweep* itself: cold start and the fallback milestone are apply-directly because there's a whole-backlog view backing the decision, while a new phase in an established roadmap is propose-only. A filing path has a narrower view still — one finding, not the whole backlog — so even the sweep's own "apply directly" cases (cold-starting the roadmap, creating the fallback) are out of bounds for a filer. The rule is deliberately binary rather than "usually shouldn't": pick among what already exists, or file without one. This composes with (rather than duplicates) the worker-side prohibition `milestone-prohibition.md` already states for a `/shipyard:do-work` worker's follow-up-issue filings — that fragment's existing carve-out ("a worker filing a follow-up issue may stamp it with the parent issue's own existing milestone") is the same shape as `/decompose-epic`'s parent→child inheritance below, generalized here to the non-worker filing paths (`shipyard:filing-github-issues`, `/shipyard:file-issue`) that don't have a "parent issue" to inherit from at all.

**Why "never fail a filing over a milestone" is load-bearing, not just polite.** The dominant caller of `shipyard:filing-github-issues` is an unattended `*-auditor` agent filing findings in bulk — an audit run producing 30 findings with a flaky `gh api .../milestones` call (rate limit, transient 5xx, a repo with zero milestones because `/shipyard:init` hasn't cold-started the roadmap yet) must not turn into 30 lost findings. Every failure mode in the milestone-lookup path (empty list, API error, no matching phase, absent fallback) resolves to "file without a milestone, note it" — never to skipping the `gh issue create` call itself. This is the same fail-open posture the block's own schema description already commits to ("Never fail a filing over a milestone... file the issue anyway without one, and note it").

**Why the milestone list is cached once per run, not fetched per finding.** Re-deriving this from `shipyard:filing-github-issues`' existing "Discover labels once" convention (the skill already caches `gh label list` once per run for the same reason) — an audit run filing 30 findings against the same repo has one stable, unchanging answer to "what milestones exist," so re-fetching per finding is 29 wasted API calls for the marginal one that matters.

**Why `--milestone` takes a title, not a number.** `gh issue create --milestone` resolves by milestone title string, not numeric ID — this sidesteps needing to resolve a milestone's numeric ID at all in the filing paths (unlike `/decompose-epic`'s existing `addSubIssue` GraphQL call, which does need node IDs for a different reason). The matching logic only ever needs to carry a milestone's `title` forward from the cached list to the `gh issue create` call.

**Why sub-issue inheritance in `/decompose-epic` is unconditional on the parent, not re-matched against `BET:`.** A sub-issue produced by decomposition is, by definition, already scoped to the same work the parent epic represents — re-running the `BET:` match against the parent's own milestone list would either agree with the parent's existing assignment (redundant) or disagree with it (a worker second-guessing a placement decision it has no standing to make, since `milestone-prohibition.md` already forbids a worker from making milestone *decisions*, only from inheriting filing metadata). Making inheritance unconditional — read the parent's `milestone.title` once, pass it through to every child, no matching logic — removes the only case where a decomposition worker would need to reason about phases at all, matching the issue body's own framing: *"Make it require no judgment at all: a worker-filed issue inherits its parent issue's milestone."* If the parent has no milestone, children get none either — there is nothing to infer, and inferring one would be the exact roadmap-authorship act workers are barred from.

**Why `commands/refine-issues.md` shipped with zero code changes despite being named in the issue's original surface list.** The issue's "Surfaces to cover" section named four things, including `commands/refine-issues.md`. A `gh issue create` / `gh api ... -X POST .../issues` grep across that file returns zero matches — `/refine-issues` only ever rewrites the body and labels of an issue that already exists (the classify+rewrite and resolve-defaults branches both mutate in place); it never files a new one. There is therefore no filing call site in that file for `assign_on_file` to gate, and no milestone-inheritance concept applicable (rewriting a body doesn't touch the milestone field GitHub already carries on the issue). This was confirmed by grep before writing any diff, per the issue-work "confirm the problem the body describes actually exists" verification-first stance, rather than assumed from the issue text. The orchestrator's own dispatch-time scope framing for this issue independently narrowed the surface list to `commands/file-issue.md` + `skills/filing-github-issues/SKILL.md` + `commands/refine-issues.md` + "the decompose paths," dropping the five additional call sites (`commands/audit.md`, `commands/audit-scheduled.md`, `commands/eas-watch.md`, `commands/optimize-markdown.md`, and the deferred-slice filing path in `agents/issue-worker/issue-work.md`) a later comment on the issue had identified — `commands/audit.md`/`audit-scheduled.md` are covered transitively (every auditor files through `shipyard:filing-github-issues`, so wiring the shared skill covers all 18 of them and both dispatch commands with a single edit), and the remaining three (`eas-watch.md`, `optimize-markdown.md`, the deferred-slice worker path) are left for a follow-up rather than expanded into this dispatch's scope.

## End-of-loop milestone roadmap sweep — why it's non-fatal, why it skips on a no-op loop, and why the skill's own step-7 report is suppressed here (issue #1243)

**Decision: wire `shipyard:update-roadmap`'s Procedure steps 1–6 into `/do-work`'s own end-of-session cleanup ([`cleanup-summary.md`'s step 8.5](./do-work/cleanup-summary.md#end-of-session-cleanup)), gated on `milestones.enabled` AND `milestones.sweep_on_loop_end` both `true`, and fold its tallies into the existing end-of-session summary rather than let the skill post its own separate report.**

**Why this belongs in `cleanup-summary.md`, not a standalone dispatch.** A `/do-work` loop is precisely the event that makes a roadmap stale — it closes issues in bulk, opens PRs, and files new ones (auditor findings, deferred slices, decomposed epics), all of which arrive with no milestone. The sweep needs to run at the point the session's own artifacts (`shipped_count`, `filed_count`) are already known, which is after drain and reap, immediately before the summary renders — not as a separate command a human has to remember to run afterward.

**Why non-fatal.** A roadmap sweep is hygiene, not correctness — an issue that stays unmilestoned for one more session is a minor rank-ordering gap (`milestones.prioritize_dispatch`, issue #1241, degrades gracefully to the plain P0>P1>P2 fallback for anything unplaced), not a data-loss or safety concern. Gating the loop's exit status on it would let an unrelated `gh` API hiccup (rate limit, transient 5xx) or a sweep-side bug fail an otherwise-successful session — a worse trade than losing the sweep for one run. The failure is caught and folded into a one-line summary advisory instead (see [cleanup-summary.md](./do-work/cleanup-summary.md#end-of-session-summary)'s `Roadmap sweep (#1243):` line rule).

**Why skip cleanly when the loop did nothing.** A drain-only or no-op session (nothing shipped, nothing filed) has, by construction, produced no new unmilestoned artifact this run — there's nothing for a loop-end sweep to catch up on that wasn't already true before the session started. Running it anyway burns a full backlog read (`gh issue list --state open --limit 500`, plus a milestones fetch) for zero marginal benefit. The skip condition is `shipped_count + filed_count == 0`, reusing the same two counters [`cleanup-summary.md`](./do-work/cleanup-summary.md)'s own "Write the consolidated report to disk" step already keys its skip condition on (`shipped_count + filed_count + reaped_worktrees == 0`).

**Why the skill's own step-7 report is suppressed here.** `shipyard:update-roadmap`'s own [Procedure](../skills/update-roadmap/SKILL.md#procedure) step 7 posts a summary as a GitHub comment (or prints one standalone). Doing that AND also folding the same tallies into `/do-work`'s end-of-session summary would be two reports describing the identical run — the issue's own constraint calls this out explicitly ("fold the sweep's Applied/Proposed/Flagged output into the existing end-of-loop summary; do not emit a second report … a separate report at the end of a long loop is a report nobody reads"). This integration therefore runs steps 1–6 of the skill's procedure (gate, fetch, cold-start check, unmilestoned sweep, dependency sweep, honesty checks) and skips step 7, routing the same Applied/Proposed tallies into `/do-work`'s own summary line instead.

**Why this dispatch does NOT implement the issue body's later "start-of-session sweep" design.** The issue body was substantially expanded after initial filing to describe a two-pass design — a narrow, mechanical start-of-session placement pass (so a stale-unmilestoned backlog doesn't corrupt `prioritize_dispatch`'s ranking) plus the fuller end-of-session hygiene pass this dispatch implements. The issue's own **Acceptance** section, however, states only the end-of-session criteria ("Issues filed during a loop carry a milestone by the time the loop reports … a sweep failure leaves the loop's exit status unchanged … nothing runs when the feature is disabled"), and the orchestrator's dispatch-time scope note for this issue narrowed the ask to exactly that plus the audit-command decision below. The start-of-session pass is a distinct, independently-shippable piece of work — it fires at a different point in the session lifecycle, with different constraints (mechanical placement only, no proposals, no honesty checks) — rather than a small addition to this PR's diff, so it's left as a natural follow-up instead of being folded into this dispatch's scope.

**Why `/shipyard:audit` does NOT get the same auto-wired sweep.** The issue's closing paragraph raises the identical staleness concern for any bulk-filing surface, naming `/shipyard:audit` explicitly, and offers two options: wire the same sweep into `audit.md`'s own end-of-run summary, or document the gap and assign it to the operator. This lands on the latter. An audit run is typically much shorter than a `/do-work` loop and files far fewer issues per run — each `*-auditor` agent files a handful of findings, not the dozens a multi-hour dispatch loop can close+open+file — so the sweep's own backlog-read cost is proportionally larger relative to the audit's own runtime, and duplicating the gate/skip/non-fatal/fold-into-summary machinery this section documents into a second command for a thinner benefit isn't a good trade. [`audit.md`'s "End-of-run summary" section](./audit.md#end-of-run-summary) now states this explicitly, pointing the operator at `/shipyard:update-roadmap` (or its `/loop 1d` periodic cadence) as the remediation.

## Enforcing the merged-PR-is-not-worker-done invariant (issue #1237)

**Decision: a mechanical gate inside `worktree-reap.sh` itself — `reap --action reaped` on an `agent-*` worktree refuses unless the owning agent-id is already recorded as returned in this session's persisted state, or the caller passes an explicit `--bypass-return-check <reason>`.** Not a config knob, not an orchestrator-side "remember to check" instruction: the destructive removal is the single choke point every reap call site already routes through (issue #284's own rationale for `worktree-reap.sh reap` as the sole audit-log writer), so gating there closes the invariant for every call site at once, present and future, rather than relying on each new call site's author remembering to re-derive the check by hand.

**The repro this closes (issue #1235).** This session's own orchestrator observed PR #1233 at `state: MERGED` with 5 green checks while the `fix-checks-only` worker dispatched against it was still running its own post-push verification — and treated `MERGED` as the reconcile signal: it cleared `.in_flight` and reaped the worker's worktree before the worker had actually returned. Both existing safety nets were structurally unreachable from that path: `.in_flight` membership (issue #832) had already been cleared by the same premature reconcile that triggered the reap, and issue #838's pre-reap unpushed-work inspection is never reached because the orchestrator believed the slot was already free — there was no "about to reap, let me check for unpushed work" moment, because the reap wasn't recognized as premature at all. #1238 landed the advisory half of the fix (a `dont.md` bullet, restatements at steady-state.md's step A and step B, a RATIONALE narrative). This issue is the enforceable half: make the ordering a property the SCRIPT verifies, not a property the orchestrator's own reasoning has to get right on every turn.

**Why this had to live in `worktree-reap.sh`, not in steady-state.md's prose.** An LLM orchestrator's protocol discipline is advisory by construction — the #1235 repro is proof a documented sequence (A.0 → A.1 → B, reap only after parsing a terminal return) can still be short-circuited by an in-context judgment call that looks locally reasonable ("the PR merged, the checks are green, this is obviously done"). A rule that lives only in markdown the orchestrator reads and reasons from can be reasoned past the same way; a rule enforced by the one script every reap call site is already required to route through cannot be, regardless of which turn or which shortcut the orchestrator's own reasoning takes to get there.

**Why `reconciled_agent_ids` itself could not be the gate signal, unmodified.** `reconciled_agent_ids` is documented (`orchestrator-state-reference.md`) as session-local working memory, written at the end of A.1's per-mode handling — not persisted, and not available to `worktree-reap.sh`, which runs as a separate bash process with no access to the orchestrating model's own memory. The fix adds a narrower, persisted counterpart, `.returned_agent_ids` (session-state.sh schema, issue #1237), written by a NEW block at the TOP of A.1 — immediately after the return string is available, before any per-mode branch (including the same-turn reap calls those branches make) runs. Moving the write earlier than `reconciled_agent_ids`'s own "end of A.1" timing is deliberate: A.1's own `shipped`-immediate reap and step B's per-completion reap both fire DURING per-mode handling, so gating on a record written only at the very end of A.1 would make even the legitimate same-turn reaps fail the gate they're supposed to pass.

**Why A.0.5 could not be gated the same way as every other call site.** A.0.5 is the crash-recovery / stalled-worker reap — it fires precisely when a worker has NOT produced a terminal return (a crash, a stall-watchdog kill, or an exhausted resume). By definition, an agent A.0.5 reaps never wrote a `.returned_agent_ids` entry and structurally never will — requiring one there would not add a safety margin, it would break crash recovery outright, which is the opposite of a hardening. A.0.5's reap call carries an explicit `--bypass-return-check "crash-recovery reap (#1237) — worker never reached a terminal return"` instead: the exception is a decision visible in the diff and in the audit log, not a silent carve-out.
