Since [#1291](https://github.com/mattsears18/shipyard/issues/1291) that call lives inside [`scripts/crash-recovery-reap.sh`](../scripts/crash-recovery-reap.sh) rather than in `steady-state.md`'s prose — see the closing paragraph of this section for why the flag is threaded at the script's internal call rather than exposed on its own command line.

**Call-site classification — every `worktree-reap.sh reap` invocation, gated or exempt and why:**

| Call site | `--action` | Worktree-name shape | Gated or exempt | Why |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| `crash-recovery-reap.sh` (steady-state.md A.0.5's crash-recovery reap, extracted by #1291) | `reaped` | `agent-*` | **Exempt** (internal `--bypass-return-check`) | The reaped agent never returned, by definition — the whole premise of A.0.5. The script only reaps on its `terminal=false` branch, so every reap it performs is this case. |
| steady-state.md A.1 `shipped`-immediate reap | `reaped` | `agent-*` | **Gated, satisfied naturally** | A.1's new top-of-step write records the return before this branch runs, same turn. |
| steady-state.md step B (per-completion reap) | `reaped` | `agent-*` | **Gated, satisfied naturally** | Runs strictly after A.1 in the same turn; the write already landed. |
| dispatch-rules.md §2d (pre-dispatch head-branch reap) | `reaped` | `agent-*` | **Exempt** (internal `--bypass-return-check`, added issue #1274) | ~~Originally classified "gated, satisfied naturally" on the assumption the target's originating worker was reconciled in an earlier turn of the SAME session.~~ That assumption doesn't hold: `failed_prs` comes from an `--author @me` scan (setup/04-backlog-divert.md step 5), not from this session's own `session_prs` — step 5.7's "inherited DIRTY PR" seeding is the spec's own precedent that a PR (and the local worktree that opened it) can be inherited from a PRIOR session, whose `.returned_agent_ids` record lives in a session-state file this session's `--session-id` can never read. Reproduced live (issue #1274): the gate refused with `"reap-refused"/"no-recorded-return"` while the wrapper script still reported `reaped=true` and the worktree stayed on disk — the exact "per-completion reap doesn't succeed" symptom the issue investigates. The bypass is safe because this call site's own precondition (a PR already in `failed_prs`) proves the worker's deliverable already exists on the remote. |
| drain.md's mirror of §2d | `reaped` | `agent-*` | **Exempt** (internal `--bypass-return-check`, added issue #1274) | Same correction and reasoning as dispatch-rules.md §2d above — the drain phase's PR set is likewise not restricted to this session's own dispatches. |
| cleanup-summary.md targeted pass (`reap-session-worktrees`) — **removed; harness sweep** | `reaped` (internal) | `agent-*` | **Exempt** (internal `--bypass-return-check`) | Its own documented contract unions `reconciled_agent_ids` with any still-`in_flight` agent-id as a defensive catch-all for an incompletely-released slot — the latter may legitimately not have returned. |
| cleanup-summary.md generic straggler sweep (step 3.1) | `reaped` | `agent-*` | **Exempt** (`--bypass-return-check`) | By its own documentation, targets exactly what the targeted pass didn't already reach — cross-session leftovers and any this-session id the union above missed. No guarantee either way. |
| setup/01c / 00b-parallelization-cache.md (`reap-stale`) | `reaped` (internal) | `agent-*` | **Exempt** (internal `--bypass-return-check`) | Cross-session sweep — a reaped worktree may belong to a different, already-dead session whose own state file this process never reads. No single `--session-id` applies. |
| cleanup-summary.md's own-orchestrator reap | `reaped` | `orchestrator-<session-id>` | **Out of scope, not exempt** | Doesn't match `agent-*`, so the gate never fires — there is no "did an agent return" question to ask about the orchestrator reaping its own worktree. |
| setup/01d (orphan-orchestrator sweep) | `reaped-orphan-orchestrator` | `orchestrator-*` | **Out of scope** | Different action entirely, untouched by this change. |
| Every `--action deferred` call site | `deferred` | n/a | **Out of scope** | Never removes anything; the gate only guards the destructive `reaped` action. |

**Fail-closed on ambiguity, matching issue #1206's precedent.** `agent_return_recorded()` (worktree-reap.sh) treats a missing session file, a missing `.returned_agent_ids` key, and a `session-state.sh` call failure identically — all three collapse to "cannot prove the agent returned," and all three refuse the reap. This mirrors `classify-lock`'s own `unknown` verdict (an unparseable lock is NOT treated as safe-to-reap) rather than the alternative of treating a lookup failure as permissive. A refused reap performs no removal, writes a `"action":"reap-refused"` audit-log line, and returns exit 1 — every spec call site already wraps its reap call in `2>/dev/null || true`, so a refusal degrades to "the worktree is left in place for a later, correctly-gated sweep," never an aborted turn.

**Why an explicit `--bypass-return-check <reason>` flag over a baked-in phase allowlist.** An earlier shape considered was letting the script special-case known-exempt `--phase` values (`reconcile-A.0.5`, `setup-3b`, `cleanup-session-targeted`) internally. Rejected: `--phase` is an audit-log label, not a decision the caller visibly makes — baking exemption logic into a lookup table the caller never touches recreates exactly the kind of implicit, easy-to-forget-about carve-out this issue exists to replace with something auditable. Requiring an explicit flag with a human-readable reason means every exemption is a line in the diff a reviewer sees, and a reason string a future auditor can grep for — visible and deliberate, per the issue's own framing of what "enforceable" should mean.

**Where A.0.5's bypass actually lives, after #1291's extraction.** [#1291](https://github.com/mattsears18/shipyard/issues/1291) moved A.0.5's ~420-line crash-recovery block out of `steady-state.md` and into [`scripts/crash-recovery-reap.sh`](../scripts/crash-recovery-reap.sh), so the `worktree-reap.sh reap --action reaped` call this section exempts is no longer a spec-prose call site — it is a line inside that script. The bypass is therefore threaded at the script's own internal call, with a fixed reason string, rather than exposed as a `crash-recovery-reap.sh reap` flag its caller (`steady-state.md`'s A.0.5) would have to remember to pass. That's the same shape `reap-stale` and `reap-session-worktrees` already use for their internal `reap_action` calls, and it's correct here for a stronger reason than convenience: `crash-recovery-reap.sh` reaps ONLY on its `terminal=false` branch — the `terminal=true` path returns early having reaped nothing — so *every* reap the script performs is by construction a crash-recovery reap of an agent that never reached a terminal return. A caller-supplied flag would add a way to get it wrong without adding a case where a different answer is ever correct. The exception remains equally visible in the diff and equally greppable in the audit log; it just lives one file deeper, next to the call it exempts.

## Why no structural follow-up to #1380's in-process DIRTY resolver (issue #1381)

**Decision: do nothing further. The in-process resolver ([#1380](https://github.com/mattsears18/shipyard/pull/1380), direction 4) is the complete answer to [#1377](https://github.com/mattsears18/shipyard/issues/1377); none of the three structural alternatives is worth building on top of it.** Full analysis in [`docs/design/issue-1381-version-coordination-followups.md`](../../../docs/design/issue-1381-version-coordination-followups.md); the three load-bearing reasons, so a future reader doesn't have to re-derive them:

1. **Two of the three directions can't be built on this repo at all.** Land-time bump (direction 1) and placeholder + merge-time resolve (direction 2) both require an automation to write a commit to `main` after a merge. This repo's `main` ruleset (id `16669759`) has `bypass_actors: []` alongside a `pull_request` rule and five required status checks — so no actor, including a GitHub Actions `GITHUB_TOKEN` or a repo admin, can push there. The two workarounds are both worse than the residual they'd remove: adding a bypass actor recreates the ungated-write-path-to-`main` shape [`scripts/detect-ungated-admin-direct-merge.sh`](../scripts/detect-ungated-admin-direct-merge.sh) and #438 / #465 / #598 / #602 / #645 / #716 exist to prevent, and "the resolver opens a second PR" is the `do-work/changelog-backfill-<M>` mechanism #691 already built and deleted for PR-volume churn.

2. **Direction 2's premise is measurably false, and adopting it would regress #1380.** A scratch-repo git experiment (recorded in the design doc) shows two branches each prepending a *byte-identical* `### UNRELEASED — <date>` heading with differing body prose **still conflict** — and produce a strictly worse shape than today's: git merges the shared heading and conflicts inside the entry body, leaving one heading with two competing bodies whose correct resolution is a restructure, not a concatenation. That shape fails `scripts/resolve-manifest-only-dirty.sh`'s `changelog-conflict-outside-top-insert` gate (both sides' hunk content must start with `### `), so every collision would fall through to a full `fix-rebase` dispatch — reinstating the ~1.11M-token cost #1377 measured and #1380 eliminated. Only the manifest `.version` row genuinely auto-merges under an identical placeholder.

3. **#691 is direct precedent.** Direction 2 introduces a token whose value is unknown at write time and patched in later by automation — exactly the mechanism #691 removed on a maintainer decision after it generated five bug issues (#581, #583, #690, #700, #704) plus per-PR churn. The decision recorded there (eliminate the unknown-at-write-time value rather than build machinery to resolve it) applies unchanged with `version` substituted for `PR #`.

**On direction 3 specifically** (`changelog.d/` fragments): it does **not** re-introduce #691's backfill machinery — post-#691 fragment content is authored complete and the version heading is authored by the assembler, so nothing is written-then-patched. It fails for different reasons: it leaves the manifest `.version` row (the majority of the measured cost) untouched, and its assembly step either hits the same protected-`main` wall (if assembled `CHANGELOG.md` stays committed) or breaks the three consumers that read the file directly — `changelog-monotonicity-scan.sh`, `conflict-marker-scan.sh`, and the required `conflict-markers.yml` check (if it becomes a generated artifact).

**What the residual actually is, after direction 4.** The resolver is wired at both DIRTY-handling call sites ([`drain.md`](./do-work/drain.md) per-poll action 2 and [`steady-state.md`](./do-work/steady-state.md)'s mid-session `merge == "DIRTY"` branch), runs in an ephemeral detached worktree at zero model cost, and defers to `fix-rebase` only on a genuinely wider conflict. What's left is one CI re-run per collision — paid in self-hosted runner minutes, not tokens. The relevant comparison is not "residual vs. zero" but "residual vs. the cost of removing it," and on this repo that cost includes weakening the `main` ruleset. The same judgment CLAUDE.md already applies in declining `strict_required_status_checks_policy: true` applies here.

**Independent re-discovery via #1456 — same conclusion reached twice, and why the measuring session still paid the cost.** [#1456](https://github.com/mattsears18/shipyard/issues/1456), filed from a *later* session (`do-work-20260817T212803Z-7058`, 2026-08-17) that itself paid ~465k tokens across three `fix-rebase` dispatches plus one wasted `fix-checks-only` dispatch on this exact manifest/CHANGELOG-only DIRTY shape, independently proposed the same four directions as #1377/#1381 above, numbered differently but substantively identical: its direction 1 ("defer the version bump out of the PR; let the merge assign it") is #1381's direction 1 (land-time bump); its direction 3 (`changelog.d/` fragments) is #1381's direction 3; its direction 2 ("let the orchestrator resolve the two-row conflict inline instead of dispatching a worker") is precisely what #1380 already shipped as direction 4; and its direction 4 (accept and document) is what this section already did. Verifying #1456's own table against the session's PR/commit history confirmed the token figures and the "every conflict confined to exactly two rows" observation were accurate — the measurement itself was not the problem.

**Why a session running well after 2026-08-14 (when #1380 landed, at plugin version 4.35.5) still paid the pre-#1380 cost is not established with certainty, but the most plausible explanation is dogfooding staleness, not a defect in the resolver itself.** `main`'s tip had advanced past `4.42.1` by the time this session ran — well beyond #1380's `4.35.5` — but the *committed* version on `main` proves only what's in the repository, not what spec content the orchestrator's own primary checkout had actually fetched at session start. This is exactly the class of bug [#1386](https://github.com/mattsears18/shipyard/issues/1386)/[#1387](https://github.com/mattsears18/shipyard/pull/1387) (the step-0.41 staleness GATE, `setup/00i-staleness-gate.md`) exists to close: a dogfooding session can reason from a primary checkout that is meaningfully behind `origin/main` while every *other* signal (issue numbers, the manifest version other sessions have pushed) suggests otherwise — #1386's own repro is a session that filed a P1 issue asserting a claim that had already shipped, the identical shape as #1456 re-measuring an already-eliminated cost. #1387 landed the same day as the #1456-filing session, closing the general staleness-detection gap; no code or spec change is warranted here beyond recording the connection, since the fix (verify the primary checkout is fresh before trusting what it says) already exists and does not need to be re-derived per-symptom.

**Disposition: no new work.** #1456 closes as independently-confirmed-already-resolved. Its own directions 1–4 collapse onto the identical four already evaluated by #1377/#1380/#1381; nothing about the later measurement changes the conclusion recorded above.

### `CLAUDE_PLUGIN_ROOT` preamble — why two layers, and why not a helper script (issues #883 / #1386)

Cold historical rationale for [`setup/00-config-worktree.md`'s step 0.3](./do-work/setup/00-config-worktree.md#03-claude_plugin_root-re-export-preamble-every-bash-tool-call). Relocated out of that file by [#1386](https://github.com/mattsears18/shipyard/issues/1386) to make room for the step-0.41 staleness gate under the setup/ per-`Read` token-budget cap (`scripts/tests/setup-phase-file-token-budget.test.sh`, 60,000 bytes) — the decisions are unchanged, only their storage location. Step 0.3 keeps the operative two-layer semantics; this is the archaeology behind them.

**Warn-on-approach threshold (issue [#1430](https://github.com/mattsears18/shipyard/issues/1430)).** `setup-phase-file-token-budget.test.sh` fails a `setup/` sub-file above the 60,000-byte cap as before, but now also **warns, non-fatally**, once a file crosses 57,000 bytes (95% of cap) — printing the remaining headroom in bytes. This exists purely to change *when* a worker learns a file is close to the wall: without it, the first sign of trouble is a CI failure on a PR that already made an ordinary, well-scoped edit (two separate PRs — #1427 and #1428 — independently tripped the hard cap on `06c-scope-handling-ui.md` in the same session before this fix). The warn threshold does not change the cap itself, and does not fail CI on its own — a file in the warn band still passes the suite. As of this writing, `00-config-worktree.md` (59,006 bytes), `01-repo-recovery.md` (58,724 bytes), and `06c-scope-handling-ui.md` (58,735 bytes, after [#1427](https://github.com/mattsears18/shipyard/issues/1427) relocated ~5KB of prose out of it) all sit inside the warn band — evidence the threshold is catching real near-misses across the cluster, not tuned to a single outlier. Splitting any of those files is tracked separately, not part of #1430's scope.

**Collapsed from four layers to two ([#883](https://github.com/mattsears18/shipyard/issues/883)).** The preamble used to also glob `$HOME/.claude/plugins/marketplaces/*/plugins/shipyard` as a third layer, hardened against a shadowing `.bak` sibling and a version-mismatched marketplace checkout ([#681](https://github.com/mattsears18/shipyard/issues/681), [#417](https://github.com/mattsears18/shipyard/issues/417)) — that layer only ever mattered when `installed_plugins.json` was unreadable or missing the `shipyard@shipyard` entry. In practice `installed_plugins.json` is populated by the harness's own plugin manager for every install method (marketplace-add-then-install, dev-linked, or otherwise) — verified directly against this maintainer's own install (`installed_plugins.json`'s `installPath` resolves to the loaded `cache/shipyard/shipyard/<version>/` directory with a real `scripts/` subdir) before this collapse landed, per the [#883 decision comment](https://github.com/mattsears18/shipyard/issues/883)'s explicit "verify layer 2 resolves across install methods before deleting layer 3" instruction. Removing the marketplace-glob layer drops ~350 bytes per occurrence (~696 → ~345 for the fallback-only bytes, ~403 total with the surrounding guards) while keeping the one layer (`installed_plugins.json`) that's both authoritative and universal. The residual risk — `installed_plugins.json` itself missing or corrupted — degrades to the repo-local-anyway fallback (a meaningful, if likely-missing, error path) rather than a silent wrong pick; this is the same fail-safe posture the old layer 4 already had, just reached one layer sooner.

**Helper-script extraction was proposed and rejected — do not re-propose it.** [#883](https://github.com/mattsears18/shipyard/issues/883) was originally scoped as "ship `scripts/resolve-plugin-root.sh`, replace each of the 66 inlined copies with a 2-line `source` call." A scoping pass rejected this: every occurrence of this preamble is an ad-hoc Bash-tool block executed in a **fresh, hermetic subshell** — nothing persists between calls (the same [#354](https://github.com/mattsears18/shipyard/issues/354) constraint step 0.3's opening paragraph documents). To `source "$CLAUDE_PLUGIN_ROOT/scripts/resolve-plugin-root.sh"`, a block must first *locate* that script — which requires the identical repo-local/installed-path probe the helper was meant to replace. The circularity is the general case, not a corner case: there is no shortcut that lets a fresh subshell find a helper script without re-deriving `$CLAUDE_PLUGIN_ROOT` first. A resolve-once-and-cache-to-disk scheme would sidestep the circularity but is a materially different, higher-risk design (cross-session and concurrent-worker cache collisions, invalidation) that doesn't help a freshly-dispatched worker's first call — the scoped fix was shrinking the inlined form in place, not extracting it.

## `needs-triage` retirement (issue #1120)

Cold detail behind [`04d-investigate-routing.md` § "Label retirement — completed"](./do-work/setup/04d-investigate-routing.md#label-retirement--completed-2026-08-15-1120). The live rule lives there; this is the archive.

### The two-condition gate that was overridden

The retirement gate committed alongside #1090 required **both** of:

1. **Migration window elapsed: earliest retirement date `2026-09-20`.** Derived from #1090's close date (`2026-08-08T00:02:32Z`) plus a window mirroring the [#520](https://github.com/mattsears18/shipyard/issues/520) `needs-refinement` precedent — #520 (the code change that made *that* label unnecessary) closed `2026-06-11T05:11:37Z`, and the label object was not deleted until [#859](https://github.com/mattsears18/shipyard/issues/859) closed `2026-07-25T04:49:13Z`, a **43-day** gap. The same 43 days applied to #1090's close date gives `2026-09-20`. The section said verbatim: *"Do not retire before this date regardless of how the live-usage condition below looks."*
2. **Live-usage condition: `needs-triage` is no longer the *sole* entry signal anywhere it's checked** — zero open usage, or every sampled open match also matching a detection signal.

Condition 2 held throughout. Condition 1 was waived on 2026-08-15 by a maintainer decision recorded on the issue with the `<!-- do-work-decision-resolved -->` sentinel, 36 days early.

### Why the date was waivable and the usage check was not

The date was never evidence of anything — it was one precedent's interval copied onto a second, unrelated close date. What it actually encodes is a **risk-tolerance choice about unmeasurable third-party installs**: how long to leave a compatibility carve-out in place for consuming repos that have not upgraded and whose state shipyard cannot observe. That is a judgment call belonging to the maintainer, not a fact a session can verify — which is exactly why two prior `/do-work` dispatches (2026-08-08, 2026-08-09) were **correct** to decline to make it and return `blocked` on the gate. The live-usage condition is the opposite: a measurable property, re-measurable cheaply on demand, and therefore never a candidate for waiver.

The generalizable lesson: a spec gate mixing a *measurable* condition with a *risk-tolerance* condition should say which is which, so a session can tell what it is allowed to evaluate and what it must escalate. A worker that cannot distinguish them either over-defers (burning a dispatch re-deriving a date, as the 2026-08-09 run did at ~160k tokens) or over-reaches.

### Blast radius — every writer that had to move first

Deleting a label object while something still writes to it silently recreates it. The retirement therefore re-pointed every writer before the delete:

| Writer | Kind | Disposition |
|---|---|---|
| `setup/01c-label-recovery-refine.md` | create | deleted |
| `setup/00b-parallelization-cache.md` | create | deleted |
| `skills/filing-github-issues/SKILL.md` | create + apply | section removed; filers route to `needs-human-review` |
| `agents/dx-auditor.md` | apply | `Needs triage: yes` items now take `needs-human-review` |
| `skills/dx-catalog/SKILL.md` | apply | same |
| `agents/observability-auditor.md` | apply | vendor-choice findings take `needs-human-review` |
| `commands/file-issue.md` | apply | gate-label guidance narrowed to `needs-human-review` |

`/shipyard:refine-issues` was **not** a writer despite the repo-root `CLAUDE.md` claiming it was — its escalate branch has routed to `needs-human-review` since [#520](https://github.com/mattsears18/shipyard/issues/520). That stale bullet was corrected in the same PR.

### Where the `Needs triage: yes` catalog flag went

`dx-catalog`'s four `Needs triage: yes` items (and `observability-auditor`'s vendor-choice finding) marked findings whose *resolution* needs a human to pick between real alternatives — a vendor, a paid tier, a policy. Under the binary backlog that is `needs-human-review` by definition (a genuine decision, not a mechanical gap), so the flag now maps there. It is deliberately **not** mapped to `agent-console`: choosing a vendor is a decision, not a console action an agent could drive.

### Config-key retirement followed #1360

`labels.needs_triage` was removed from the built-in defaults, the JSON schema, and this repo's `shipyard.config.json`. This is required rather than cosmetic: [step 1.75](./do-work/setup/01e-verify-config-labels.md#175-verify-config-named-labels-exist-in-the-target-repo-1359) verifies every config-named label exists as a real label object in the target repo, so leaving the key while deleting the object would make the check flag a phantom label on every session. [#1360](https://github.com/mattsears18/shipyard/issues/1360) set the precedent by retiring `blocked` / `blocked_hard` the same way for the same reason.

## `version_cursor` self-heal — why `reseed-if-idle` is a separate, once-per-round call, and why full claim-tracking (option 1) was deferred (issue #1417)

[Issue #1417](https://github.com/mattsears18/shipyard/issues/1417) found that `next-available-version.sh compute` advances the session-local `version_cursor` to the version it **computed**, not the version a worker actually **claimed**. Those diverge whenever a worker legitimately takes a different bump level than the orchestrator inferred — sanctioned explicitly by the dispatch prompt's "the level is yours to raise" clause (issue #671). When that happens, the cursor is left holding a slot nobody claimed, and every subsequent `compute` in the session floors above the phantom — permanent-for-the-session and compounding with each further divergence. The repro: a `feat(dispatch):` issue inferred `minor`, computing `next_available_version=4.36.0` and advancing the cursor there; the dispatched worker correctly judged the actual change was patch-scoped and shipped `4.35.19` instead. The cursor was never told, and the *next* dispatch's `compute` — by then with zero open session PRs, everything having merged — floored above the phantom `4.36.0` and inflated a full minor further, to `4.37.0`, with `origin/main` still at `4.35.19`.

### Why the fix is NOT inside `compute`'s own cursor fold-in logic

The obvious-looking fix — condition `compute`'s existing "fold in the persisted cursor" step on whether `--session-prs` has a currently-OPEN member — is unsafe and was rejected after `scripts/tests/next-available-version.test.sh`'s existing "cursor-file persistence" case proved why: that test calls `compute` **twice in a row with no `--session-prs` at all**, and asserts the second call folds in the first call's cursor write. This is not an incidental test gap — it is the literal shape of a **batch dispatch** (issue #437): [setup/07-pool-fill.md](./do-work/setup/07-pool-fill.md#7-initial-pool-fill) runs the computation **N times in sequence, against the identical `--session-prs` value**, before any of the batch's own sibling PRs exist to populate that list. If `compute` itself discarded the cursor whenever `--session-prs` had no OPEN member, every slot in a batch dispatched against a session with no other PRs yet open (the common case — the very first batch of a session) would silently recompute the identical `next_available_version`, reintroducing the exact N−1-PRs-go-DIRTY collision #437 exists to prevent. `compute`, called in isolation, cannot structurally distinguish "this is slot 2 of a live batch, trust the cursor" from "this is a fresh dispatch after the last one drained, the cursor is stale" — both present with the same `--session-prs` value and the same absent-or-present cursor file. Any fix living inside `compute` would have to guess at that distinction from data that doesn't carry it, and guessing wrong is worse than the drift being fixed (the explicit floor-direction constraint on this issue: the floor may err high, never low enough to let two PRs compute the same slot).

### Why `reseed-if-idle` is a separate, once-per-round subcommand instead

The distinguishing signal — "is this call part of an in-progress batch sequence, or a standalone dispatch decision" — is knowable to the **caller** (the orchestrator knows whether it is about to fire one dispatch or loop N times to fill a batch) but not to `compute` itself. So the self-heal lives in a new subcommand, `reseed-if-idle`, called **exactly once per dispatch-decision round**, strictly *before* whichever `compute` call follows — the single call for an ordinary per-dispatch (dispatch-rules.md's "Next-available-version computation"), or once before the batch's sequential per-slot loop (setup/07-pool-fill.md step 7), never inside that loop. Because the reseed happens once, before any of a batch's own N `compute` calls run, it cannot interfere with the monotonic advances those N calls make against each other — by the time the loop starts, the cursor is already in its correct pre-round state (reset if genuinely idle, left alone if a real claim might still exist), and `compute`'s own fold-in logic is completely unchanged. This also means the existing `next-available-version.test.sh` and `version-coordination-bump-level.test.sh` suites needed **zero** modification to their existing assertions — only new test cases for the new subcommand — which is itself evidence the change didn't touch the mechanism those tests pin.

### Why option 1 (advance-on-claim via a `record-claim` reconcile call) was deferred

The issue's preferred fix (option 1) makes the cursor mean exactly what its own documentation already claims — "the highest version anyone has *claimed*" rather than "the highest version anyone has *computed*" — by having the orchestrator's `shipped`-reconcile path read the actually-merged manifest version off the PR and call a new `next-available-version.sh record-claim --version <v> --cursor-file <p>`. This is the structurally correct fix, but it adds a **new orchestrator-side call site in the reconcile hot path** — one more mandatory step a future edit to `steady-state.md`'s A.1 return-string handling could silently drop, the same fragility class [#1399](https://github.com/mattsears18/shipyard/issues/1399)'s `ci_backpressure` invariant-line token was built to catch for a different mandatory-but-easy-to-forget step. Shipping it "half-wired" (a `record-claim` subcommand with no invariant-line evidence that it actually fired every reconcile) would trade one silent-drift bug for another, harder-to-notice one — exactly the outcome issue #1417 itself warned against. `reseed-if-idle` (option 2) plus the `version_cursor` invariant-line token (option 3, below) together close the same observable gap — a stale cursor no longer silently compounds for an entire session, and any residual drift between drain points is visible on every turn — at a fraction of the surface area, deliberately leaving option 1 as a named, scoped follow-up rather than landing it unobserved in this PR.

### `version_cursor` invariant-line token (option 3)

Added `version_cursor=<value|"unset"|n/a>` to [step E's invariant line](./do-work/steady-state.md#e-invariant-line-end-of-every-steady-state-turn), documented in [`invariant-line.md`](./do-work/invariant-line.md), mirroring the `ci_backpressure` / `disk_free_mb` precedent exactly: `n/a` when `version_coordination.enabled` is false or no `manifest_path` is configured (nothing to report, the common case for most repos); `"unset"` when coordination is enabled but no cursor has been written yet this session; otherwise the cursor file's literal value, read fresh at emission time (a cheap local file read — no `gh` call needed, unlike most of this file's other tokens). The divergence smell it exists to surface: a `version_cursor` value implausibly far ahead of `origin/<default-branch>`'s own manifest version relative to `session_prs`'s actual claims is exactly the #1417 drift, now visible on every turn instead of requiring a human to notice an implausible-looking version number by eye — which is how the original repro was caught in the first place.

## Release-on-non-claim — why a released slot becomes a hole instead of a cursor rollback, and why that needs no per-slot promise ledger (issue #1420)

[Issue #1420](https://github.com/mattsears18/shipyard/issues/1420) is the follow-up #1417 named, but it did **not** ship the fix #1417 proposed. Its body's "option 1" (`record-claim` writing the actually-shipped version, plus `compute` no longer writing the cursor) was **declined by a dispatched worker**, the decline was accepted as correct, and the maintainer re-scoped the issue by recorded decision (`<!-- shipyard-resolve-decisions -->`, 2026-08-19). What follows is the design that decision asked for, and the reasoning that lets it ship without the ledger the decline argued would otherwise be required.

### What the decline established, and the second leak that changed the answer

Two things came out of the decline analysis and are load-bearing here:

1. **`compute` must keep writing the cursor unconditionally.** A batch fill calls `compute` N times in sequence *before any of the batch's own sibling PRs exist*, so `--session-prs` is identical (often with zero OPEN members) across all N calls. The cursor write-then-read is the only thing that gives slot 2 a different answer than slot 1. Dropping it collapses every slot in a from-scratch batch to the same value — the exact [#437](https://github.com/mattsears18/shipyard/issues/437) collision. Body item 2 is therefore **not implemented**, and the raise-only contract at `dispatch-rules.md` is unchanged.
2. **An unconditional downward correction is unsafe.** `record-claim` writing the claimed value straight into the cursor can stomp headroom already *promised* to a still-in-flight, not-yet-opened sibling. Nothing about that promise is visible to a subcommand that sees only a scalar file and the one PR it is reconciling.

The re-scope came from a **second leak variant**, observed forty minutes after the first in the same session and reported in a self-correction comment on the issue: the orchestrator dispatched #1420 itself, `compute` inferred `minor` and returned `4.39.0` (advancing the cursor), and the worker then **declined** — no branch, no commit, no PR. The next dispatch read a cursor of `4.40.0` while the true highest claim across every open PR was `4.38.0`. This variant has nothing to do with bump levels: the worker never took *any* version. It survives `reseed-if-idle` too, and correctly so — two PRs (#1424, #1425) were open the whole time, which is exactly when `reseed-if-idle` must not fire. So "just tighten the raise-only contract" came off the table, and the operation both leak variants actually need was named: **release a slot whose dispatch ended without claiming it.**

### Why the release is a hole, not a cursor rollback

The decision's constraint is blunt: *"A release is not a cursor rollback… a declined slot may not be the highest one outstanding… Naively decrementing the cursor reintroduces the #437 collision."* Its suggested shape was "only collapse the cursor when the released version *is* the current top, otherwise record the hole."

Collapse-when-top turns out to be unsafe even in that narrow case, and it is worth writing down why, because the shape looks obviously fine. Take a batch fill handing out `4.48.0` / `4.49.0` / `4.50.0`, cursor at `4.50.0`. Worker 3 declines. `4.50.0` *is* the top, so a collapse fires — but collapsing means deleting or lowering the cursor, and the next `compute`'s floor is then `max(origin/main, session_prs walk)`. Workers 1 and 2 are still running with no PRs open, so the walk cannot see their promises: the floor comes back at `4.47.x` and the next dispatch is handed `4.48.0`, colliding with worker 1. **Collapsing safely requires knowing the cursor's predecessor, and knowing that requires the per-slot promise ledger.** So this implementation never collapses.

Recording the hole reaches the same outcome without the risk. The cursor stays put; the released version is appended to a holes companion file (`<cursor-file>.holes`); and the next `compute` **prefers the smallest hole** over a fresh advance, bounded two ways:

- **Strictly above `claimed_floor`** — the floor derived from *ground truth alone* (origin's manifest plus the open-PR walk), captured before the cursor is folded in. Nothing at or below that is free, so a reclaimed slot can never collide with a real claim. Holes that fall below it have been claimed in the meantime and are pruned.
- **No higher than the slot `compute` would otherwise have handed out** — so reclaiming can only ever *lower* the emitted number, never inflate past the raise-only contract's own prediction.

A hole is consumed at most once, and only ever enters the file for a dispatch that has already terminated, so it can never collide with a live promise either. In the maintainer's own "release the top" case the result is observationally identical to the collapse they described — the very next `compute` hands `4.39.0` straight back out — while the non-top case (`4.48.0` declined with `4.49.0`/`4.50.0` still promised) reclaims `4.48.0` and leaves the cursor at `4.50.0`, untouched. Both are pinned in [`next-available-version.test.sh`](../scripts/tests/next-available-version.test.sh), alongside a case asserting that with no holes file the behavior is byte-for-byte the pre-#1420 path — which is what keeps every pre-existing cursor/batch-monotonicity assertion in that suite meaningful rather than incidentally still-green.

### Why this needs no per-slot promise ledger — the asymmetry that decides it

The decline's strongest argument against `.in_flight[<slot>].promised_version` bookkeeping was not that it's hard to write, but that **its failure mode is worse than the status quo**: *"a silently under-counted floor reintroduces the exact #437 collision this whole mechanism exists to prevent, rather than merely wasting version numbers."* That objection is decisive for any design where forgetting the bookkeeping makes the floor *lower*.

It does not apply here, and the reason is the single most important property of this design: **the release never lowers the cursor, so a missing, skipped, or failed release cannot lower the floor.** It can only fail to *raise* the reclaim rate. Concretely, when `.in_flight[<slot-id>].version_slot` is absent — a dispatch that predates this change, a future edit that drops the write, a `release` call the classifier denies — the outcome is that the slot stays leaked, which is **exactly the pre-#1420 status quo**. Never a collision. The bookkeeping this ships is therefore one scalar on an entry the reconcile already reads, not a ledger with its own cleanup and staleness semantics, and its worst case is a no-op rather than a regression. The decision's escalation clause (*"if the release hook cannot be made safe without per-slot promise bookkeeping, STOP and file the ledger as its own issue"*) is not triggered.

What the ledger would still buy — bounded *downward* correction of the cursor — is only needed if raise-only is relaxed so a doc tweak can ship as `patch` instead of an inflated `minor`. The decision deliberately leaves that open and undecided, and nothing here forecloses it.

### Why the call site is step B, and why it gets its own invariant-line token

The release is a new mandatory step on the reconcile hot path — precisely the fragility class the deferral of option 1 was worried about, and the decision carries that worry forward: *"Ship it with an invariant-line token in the `ci_backpressure` family (#1399/#1414) so a skipped release is visible rather than silent."*

Two choices reduce the surface. First, the hook lives at **[step B](./do-work/steady-state.md#b-release-the-slot)**, not scattered across A.1's per-mode return branches. Step B is the single funnel every mode's terminal return passes through — A.0.5 says so in as many words (*"Step B still fires on every completion path — A.0.5 does NOT replace it"*) — so one call covers a `blocked` return, a crash-recovery reap, an explicit decline, and every disposition outcome that resolves an issue without a PR. A per-branch implementation would have needed a dozen call sites and would have grown one more with every future return shape. The sole exception is a **permission-classifier denial**, which by the dispatch ordering rule never gets an `in_flight` slot at all; [dispatch-rules.md's denial cleanup](./do-work/dispatch-rules.md#dispatch-rules-used-by-step-7-and-step-c) releases its own slot beside the worktree reap it already performs.

Second, `version_release=<n/a|none|released|skipped>` reports **whether the hook fired**, not what it decided — the same distinction `ci_backpressure=checked` draws (the check ran) from the queue depth it measured. `skipped` is the value the token exists for: it means the slot carried no recorded `version_slot`, so the dispatch-time write was dropped and this slot's version has leaked for the rest of the session. That converts the `version_cursor` token's existing divergence smell (a cursor implausibly far ahead of what `session_prs` accounts for) from a mystery into a signal with a cause attached.

Note the deliberate asymmetry with [#1414](https://github.com/mattsears18/shipyard/issues/1414)'s mechanical backstop for `ci_backpressure`: that one *blocks a dispatch* when the check is missing and a live re-read confirms the pool is saturated. There is no equivalent here, on purpose. A skipped release cannot produce an unsafe dispatch — it produces a slightly inflated version number — so blocking on it would trade a cosmetic cost for a real one. Observability is the whole remedy the failure mode warrants.

### What stayed unchanged

`reseed-if-idle` (#1419) is untouched, including its deliberate no-op when any `session_prs` member is OPEN — the decision says so explicitly, and it remains correct for the drift variant it covers. It does not clear the holes file either, which is safe: `compute` re-derives `claimed_floor` from ground truth on every call, so a hole surviving a reseed is either still genuinely free (fine to reclaim) or below the re-derived floor (pruned on sight). `compute`'s cursor fold-in stays unconditional. The raise-only contract stays as written. Body item 2 is not implemented.

### The DIRTY-resolve variant, and why it is fixed at the allocator rather than by recording a hole (issue [#1571](https://github.com/mattsears18/shipyard/issues/1571))

The two leak variants above both end with a dispatch that claimed nothing. [#1571](https://github.com/mattsears18/shipyard/issues/1571) is a third, structurally different one: the dispatch claims its slot, opens a PR, and then **loses that slot to the resolver that was supposed to preserve it**. [`scripts/resolve-manifest-only-dirty.sh`](../scripts/resolve-manifest-only-dirty.sh)'s `allocate_slot` folded the persisted cursor into its floor unconditionally (the #1539 secondary finding). But `compute` had already advanced that cursor **to this same PR's slot** when the PR was dispatched, so `max(floor, cursor)` equalled `pr_version` and `compute_next_free` returned the slot above it — the resolver counted the PR's own claim against the PR. At `--concurrency 1`, where every PR goes DIRTY the moment its predecessor merges, that burned one version number per PR: session `do-work-20260916T112450Z-93563` skipped 4.55.5 (PR #1569, resolved to 4.55.6) and 4.55.7 (PR #1570, resolved to 4.55.8) within an hour of each other.

**Why not record a hole.** The abandoned slot is exactly the shape the holes file exists for, and routing it there would have reclaimed it on some later `compute`. That is the wrong layer: a hole is a *recovery* mechanism for a slot whose owner is gone, and here the owner is still right there, still holding the number in its own manifest and CHANGELOG. Reclaiming it later would also hand it to a *different* PR, so the released sequence would still not match the order PRs actually merged in. The allocator now returns `pr_version` unchanged whenever it is still strictly above the floor, so no slot is abandoned and no hole is created — the cheapest possible fix, one comparison earlier than the machinery that would otherwise clean up after it.

**Why this does not weaken #1539.** The cursor fold is still the only path when the PR's own claim is genuinely dead — that is, when main has reached or passed `pr_version`, which is precisely the condition #1539's floor enforcement already tests for. A cursor strictly *above* `pr_version` means some later dispatch claimed higher slots, and that contention is still skipped past exactly as before. `scripts/tests/resolve-manifest-only-dirty-version-floor.test.sh` pins both directions: shape 5 (keep the live claim) and shape 6 (a dead claim reallocates above the cursor, not merely above the floor), alongside the four pre-existing #1539 shapes, whose resolved versions are byte-for-byte unchanged by this fix.

## Someday-milestone scoping gate — why it is a THIRD park mechanism, not a leaf of the event-gate taxonomy, and why the config knob lives under `backlog.*` not `milestones.*` (issue #1406)

### The repro, and why the existing mechanisms don't cover it

`mattsears18/lightwork#2691` sat in the `6 · Someday` milestone — a phase whose own description cites the same "genuinely event-gated, re-scoping buys nothing" reasoning [#4015 (lightwork)](https://github.com/mattsears18/shipyard/issues/4015)-style decisions document — and was correctly *not* time-gated (a `do-work-blocked-until` marker had been tried and explicitly reverted, per that repo's own PR #4025, because the date was invented rather than known). Every mechanism was being used exactly as designed, and the issue still burned a full scope-agent dispatch every single session — roughly fifteen consecutive times since 2026-07-19, all reaching the identical "defer, change nothing" conclusion — because nothing in `backlog-filter.sh classify`'s eligibility predicate ever reads milestone membership as an ELIGIBILITY signal (as opposed to a RANKING signal, which #1241's `milestones.prioritize_dispatch` already provides). The "Someday" placement was legible to a human reading the roadmap and invisible to the dispatcher.

The two existing self-clearing park mechanisms don't fit:

- **`do-work-blocked-until` (time-gate, #1161/#1165).** Requires a *known* future date. Inventing one for a park whose actual clearing condition is "App Store review volume crosses some threshold" is exactly the churn #1356 (event-gate) was built to stop — and lightwork#2691's own history shows a maintainer already tried and reverted this.
- **`do-work-recheck` (event-gate, #1356).** Requires a *probe* — `eval-recheck-probe.sh`'s allowlist was, at the time of #1406, `npm-view` and `gh-api` scoped to the dispatching repo only, a defensible security boundary against widening live-network probing to arbitrary URLs. An App Store customer-reviews RSS count was not expressible in that allowlist, and #1406 is explicitly the case where **no probe is expressible at all** — not a probe that hasn't been written yet. **Partly superseded by [#1496](https://github.com/mattsears18/shipyard/issues/1496),** which added the `url-json` verb: that specific RSS count *is* now expressible, but only on a repo that has explicitly listed `itunes.apple.com` in its committed `scope.recheck_probe_url_hosts` (empty by default). The Someday-milestone gate is unaffected either way — it answers the structurally different question below, and remains the right mechanism for the general "parked, nothing to probe" case.

### Why this is a THIRD mechanism, not the event-gate's "no probe available" leaf

The natural-looking alternative — treat "Someday, no probe" as a degenerate case of the event-gate (e.g. a `do-work-recheck` marker whose probe verb is `none`, always resolving to `unknown`) — was considered and rejected. `is_event_gated`/`probe_verdict` in `backlog-filter.sh` model a single admission question: *"has the specific external value this marker names changed?"*, decided by a live probe run once per session (`eval-probes`) and cached into `--probe-verdicts`. A Someday-milestone park answers a structurally different question: *"has a human moved this issue out of the park?"* — a fact about **milestone membership**, already present in the wide-fetch payload's `milestone` field (the same field #1241's ranking flags already read), requiring no live network call and no probe-cache plumbing at all. Bolting a third "always unknown, no probe configured" state onto `is_event_gated` would make one function answer two unrelated questions through the same code path — precisely the kind of implicit dual-purpose logic that produced the #332/#1194/#1120 drift `backlog-filter.sh`'s own header documents as the reason the script exists in the first place. Keeping `is_someday` as its own predicate, evaluated at its own point in `classify_one`'s if/elif chain, with its own verdict reason (`someday-milestone`, distinct and greppable per the issue's AC1), keeps each mechanism answering exactly one question.

### Why `backlog.someday_milestone`, not a key under `milestones.*`

`milestones.*` ([#1239](https://github.com/mattsears18/shipyard/issues/1239)) carries a hard, mechanically-relevant inertness contract — see [`do-work/dont.md`](./do-work/dont.md)'s guard: *"Don't read `milestones.*` anywhere outside a `milestones.enabled == true` guard."* A repo that has not opted into the whole milestone-roadmap surface (no `/shipyard:update-roadmap` sweeps, no milestone-aware ranking) must be byte-for-byte identical to a repo running a shipyard version with no `milestones` block at all. The #1406 motivating repro is exactly the shape that contract is designed around and would break if `someday_milestone` lived inside that block: **`mattsears18/lightwork` has `milestones.enabled: false`** at the time of this issue, yet the milestone itself — created and assigned by hand, not by `shipyard:update-roadmap` — is real and load-bearing. Nesting the knob under `milestones.*` would force a repo to turn on the *entire* roadmap-sequencing surface (ranking, filing-time assignment, the end-of-loop sweep) just to get one park signal respected, or would require carving a one-off exception into the `milestones.enabled` guard — either option defeats the inertness contract's whole point. Putting `someday_milestone` under `backlog.*` instead (alongside `respect_assignees`/`self_assign`, [#1248](https://github.com/mattsears18/shipyard/issues/1248)) sidesteps the conflict entirely: it is evaluated unconditionally of `milestones.enabled`/`milestones.prioritize_dispatch`, using only the `milestone` field the wide-fetch payload already carries regardless of whether milestone-aware ranking is turned on. A repo can use GitHub Milestones purely as a manual "park bucket" label — no roadmap surface, no phase numbering, no `/shipyard:update-roadmap` involvement — and still get the scoping gate.

### Why `--someday-milestone` takes the bare title, not the full `N · Title` string

`milestone_seq`'s existing prefix-parsing convention (`"6 · Someday"` → sequence `6`) exists because `shipyard:update-roadmap` renumbers phases as the backlog evolves — the numeric prefix is not a stable identifier across the milestone's lifetime, only its position at any given moment. Requiring the configured value to include that prefix would silently break the gate the next time a sweep inserted or removed an earlier phase, with no error and no signal — the exact "silent" failure class this whole issue exists to eliminate. `is_someday` therefore strips the same `^\s*[0-9]+\s*·\s*` prefix `milestone_seq` already parses, and compares only the human-readable remainder (case-insensitive, trimmed) — the configured value is the stable, human-chosen name ("Someday"), never the numbered title. The match is deliberately EXACT, not substring: a milestone titled "Someday, maybe" is a different phase from one titled "Someday," and treating them as equivalent would repeat the #332/#1194 "shorthand erases real signal" failure class for the new clause on day one.

### Why the drop was unconditional once matched at #1406 ship time — no partial re-admission path in that PR

`classify_one`'s chain originally placed `is_someday` as an unconditional drop — no probe, no calendar comparison, no interaction with `--respect-assignees` or any other flag. This mirrored #1406's stated acceptance-criteria scope: the gate needed to be greppable, opt-in, and ledger-accounted, and it needed to stop paying scope cost every session. The issue's own "Suggested direction" point 4 — re-scoping on a slow cadence (e.g. once per N days, reusing the existing diagnosis-reuse window) rather than never — was a genuinely good instinct the issue argued for at length, but implementing it required new machinery #1406 deliberately did not add: a persisted "last re-scoped at" timestamp keyed per issue, a cadence config knob, and a dispatch-time check that fires a scope-agent pass on a Someday-parked issue specifically *because* the cadence elapsed rather than because it's in `raw_backlog`. That was a different shape of work than a `classify` predicate — it touches session-state, not just the pure eligibility function — and #1406 deliberately left it out rather than half-implementing it. **This is no longer accurate as of #1422** — see the next section for the shipped cadence mechanism. `is_someday`'s drop remains unconditional in isolation; what changed is that `classify_one`'s `is_someday` *branch* now consults a second, orthogonal input (the recheck-cadence state) before deciding between a permanent-shaped drop and a one-time `eligible`.

## Someday-milestone recheck cadence — the slow re-scope mechanism, why it reuses `do-work-blocked-until`'s marker SHAPE under a distinct name, and why cheap-by-default (issue #1422, follow-up to #1406)

### The three existing cadence/recheck mechanisms this issue had to choose between

#1422's own dispatch prompt named three existing mechanisms and asked for a deliberate choice among them rather than a fourth invention:

1. **`scope.diagnosis_reuse_hours`** (default 72h) — the scope-preflight freshness window that reuses a cached scope-agent diagnosis **comment** instead of re-dispatching, keyed on comment age vs. issue `updatedAt`. Closest **mechanical** fit: it already implements "reuse a cached answer, but re-derive once stale," which is structurally the same shape this issue needs.
2. **`<!-- do-work-blocked-until: YYYY-MM-DD -->`** ([#1161](https://github.com/mattsears18/shipyard/issues/1161)/[#1165](https://github.com/mattsears18/shipyard/issues/1165)) — a self-clearing body marker the step-4 dispatch filter reads directly, re-admitting an issue to `raw_backlog` the instant the date elapses.
3. **`scope.external_dependency_recheck_days`** (default 14d) — an orchestrator-*computed* cadence value that writes an `external-dependency` defer's own `do-work-blocked-until` date (`today + N days`), so the proactive sweep doesn't re-judge an unchanged upstream dependency every session. Closest **semantic** analogue: it already answers "how often should we bother re-looking at a condition nothing can probe," which is exactly this issue's question.

None of the three was reusable as-is. `diagnosis_reuse_hours` governs reuse of a **comment that only exists after a scope agent already ran** — a Someday-parked issue, by #1406's design, never reaches scope-preflight in the first place, so there is no diagnosis comment to check the age of; the mechanism has nothing to attach to until AFTER the first escalation. `do-work-blocked-until` alone, reused as the park mechanism itself, was explicitly tried and reverted at the `mattsears18/lightwork#2691` repro this whole issue chain traces back to (PR #4025, "the date was invented") — using it to REPLACE the Someday-milestone park would reintroduce that exact anti-pattern. `external_dependency_recheck_days` is scoped to one defer class and would have required either a parallel, class-specific knob (violating "prefer extending an existing knob") or overloading its semantics to also mean "Someday-park recheck cadence," which is a different admission question (see the previous section's rejection of collapsing `is_someday` into `is_event_gated` for the identical reason).

### The shipped design: extend the do-work-blocked-until MARKER SHAPE, not its literal marker or its exact knob

The mechanism that shipped reuses mechanism 2's *shape* — a self-clearing `<!-- marker: YYYY-MM-DD -->` body marker the dispatch pipeline reads directly, computed from a cadence-days config knob the way mechanism 3 computes its own — but under a **distinct marker name**, `<!-- do-work-someday-recheck: YYYY-MM-DD -->`, and a **distinct config knob**, `backlog.someday_recheck_days` (default 30d, `0` disables). Two reasons the marker had to be distinct rather than reusing `do-work-blocked-until` literally:

- **Semantic conflation risk.** `do-work-blocked-until` answers "when does this issue become dispatch-ELIGIBLE again" — the step-4 filter reads it as a calendar gate on `raw_backlog` membership. The Someday-recheck marker answers a narrower, orthogonal question: "when should the orchestrator next bother CHECKING whether anything changed" — the issue stays permanently excluded from `raw_backlog` by `is_someday` regardless of this marker's value, except in the one `"escalate"` case where `classify` promotes it directly to `eligible` (not via the calendar clause). Had both questions shared one marker, an issue that legitimately acquired its own `do-work-blocked-until` from an unrelated defer class (e.g. a prior `external-dependency` escalation) would have its someday-recheck cadence silently hijacked by that unrelated date, or vice versa — exactly the "one marker answering two questions" failure class the previous section's rejection of folding `is_someday` into `is_event_gated` already argues against, recurring one layer down at the marker level instead of the predicate level.
- **The "moved out of Someday should immediately un-gate" invariant.** `do-work-blocked-until` is read by the step-4 filter **unconditionally of milestone** — any issue with a future marker drops as `time-gated`, regardless of what `is_someday` would say. Had the recheck cadence reused that exact marker, a human moving an issue OUT of the Someday milestone would NOT immediately re-admit it to dispatch if a stale someday-recheck marker was still future-dated — it would keep dropping as `time-gated` until that unrelated marker elapsed. The distinct marker name is read ONLY from inside `is_someday`'s own branch (see `someday_recheck_state()` in `backlog-filter.sh`), so it has zero effect the instant `is_someday` itself goes false. Milestone membership stays the single source of truth for the park; the marker only throttles how often the cadence bothers re-examining it.

### Why `classify` stays pure — the whole decision is computable from data already in the wide-fetch payload

The four-state decision (`someday_recheck_state()`: `"first-park"` / `"not-due"` / `"cheap-reset"` / `"escalate"`) needs exactly two inputs beyond the marker itself: `$today` (already a `classify` parameter) and the issue's `updatedAt` (already in the wide-fetch payload — GitHub bumps this timestamp on a body edit, a new comment, OR a label change, so a single field cheaply captures "did anything observable happen"). Both are already in hand with zero extra I/O, so the entire decision — including the date arithmetic (`marker_date - cadence_days` via jq's `strptime`/`mktime`/`strftime`, matching `time_gate_future`'s and `milestone_seq`'s own defensive `capture()`-wrapped-in-`[...]`-then-`first` idiom) — lives inside `CLASSIFY_JQ` as a pure function, preserving the header design note's "classify has zero `gh`/`npm` calls of its own, fixture-testable forever" invariant that `eval-probes`/`closed-by-healthy-pr`/`closed-by-open-pr` already established the pattern for. The WRITE side (persisting a fresh marker date, or the one-time initial marker) is genuine I/O and lives in a sibling subcommand, `someday-recheck-write`, for the identical reason those three subcommands are kept separate from `classify` — see `backlog-filter.sh`'s own header design note.

### Why cheap-by-default, and what "cheap" means concretely

The issue's own cost framing — *"the goal is not never look again, it's stop paying full scope cost every single session for an identical answer"* — argues explicitly for the CHEAP path being the default, expected-common outcome and the EXPENSIVE path (a real scope-agent dispatch) being rare. The shipped design makes this the structural default rather than a policy choice a caller has to opt into: reaching `"escalate"` requires `updatedAt` to have advanced past the date the marker was WRITTEN (derived arithmetically from the marker's own value — `write_date == marker_date - cadence_days` — so no separate "last checked" state needs to be persisted anywhere). Since a genuinely inert, still-parked issue's `updatedAt` does not move on its own, the overwhelmingly common outcome when a cadence window elapses is `"cheap-reset"` — a body-marker refresh with **zero** scope-agent cost, not even a cache-freshness check against a diagnosis comment (mechanism 1 above), because there is no diagnosis comment to check in the first place. Only observable activity — a human commenting, editing the body, or a label landing — earns the one real scope-agent pass. This is a strictly cheaper default than reusing `diagnosis_reuse_hours` would have been: that mechanism still requires fetching and inspecting a comment thread on every cache-check, where `someday_recheck_state` needs only two fields already resident in the wide-fetch payload the orchestrator already holds in memory.

### Why the cadence clock resets regardless of the escalated scope-agent's own defer class

[`06c-scope-handling-ui.md` step 4d](./do-work/setup/06c-scope-handling-ui.md#handling-each-returned-entry-fires-as-each-background-agent-completes) refreshes the someday-recheck marker after ANY defer conclusion for an escalated someday-parked issue — not only a defer that happens to cite the Someday park itself. A Someday-parked issue's `"escalate"` scope-agent pass can land on `external-dependency`, `human-decision-required`, or any other class; the milestone doesn't constrain WHY the issue still isn't ready, only that a human already decided it isn't a current priority. Without this unconditional reset, the cadence would collapse the moment it fired once: the marker would stay in its elapsed state (nothing wrote a fresh one), so the very next `classify` pass would immediately re-escalate the same issue, defeating the entire "at most once per cadence" acceptance criterion. The reset is explicitly skipped for a `ready` outcome — a Someday-parked issue a recheck found genuinely shippable proceeds to ordinary dispatch, and touching its marker there would either be moot (the issue closes) or actively wrong (suppressing the "still parked despite being shippable" signal a human might want to see again next cadence window).

### What #1422 deliberately left unimplemented

No per-repo cap on how many someday-parked issues can `"escalate"` in a single session. The issue's own framing assumes Someday parks are rare and cadence windows are wide (weeks, not hours), so the expected escalation rate is low by construction — adding a cap knob before any real session has ever demonstrated the need would be exactly the kind of speculative config-surface growth this repo's config layering already tries to avoid. If a future repo's Someday milestone accumulates enough issues that per-session escalation volume becomes a real cost, that is a concrete, evidence-backed follow-up, not a defect in this design.

## 00e literal invocations (issue #1455)

[`00e-pre-relocation-sweeps.md`'s step 0.45](./do-work/setup/00e-pre-relocation-sweeps.md#045-pre-relocation-session-state-init--the-worktree-cross-referencing-sweeps-1202) named its three sweeps by subcommand only (`worktree-reap.sh reap-orphan-orchestrators` / `sweep-stale-agents` / `triage-orphan-branches`) — **superseded: the first two subcommands were deleted in the harness-convergence Tier-2 cut and only `triage-orphan-branches` remains** —, leaving the orchestrator to reconstruct each call's required flags from prose. The flags aren't uniform across the three — `triage-orphan-branches` alone requires `--repo` and `--default-branch` on top of `--repo-root`, neither sibling sweep needs either — so a caller guessing wrong pays one refused tool call per mismatch, every session this step runs (repro: `do-work-20260817T212803Z-7058`).

**The full literal invocations already existed — just not where the orchestrator reads them in order.** [Claude Code's worktree cleanup](https://code.claude.com/docs/en/worktrees#clean-up-subagent-and-background-session-worktrees) and *`01c-label-recovery-refine.md`'s §3b (removed — the harness reaps worktrees)*/[§3c](./do-work/setup/01c-label-recovery-refine.md#3c-orphan-worktree-triage) each carry the complete, copy-pasteable `Bash` form for their own sweep, matching the exact flags `worktree-reap.sh`'s arg-parsing enforces. But [step 0.45](./do-work/setup/00e-pre-relocation-sweeps.md) is where the sweeps actually *execute* in session order (per [#1355](https://github.com/mattsears18/shipyard/issues/1355)/[#1365](https://github.com/mattsears18/shipyard/issues/1365)'s reordering) — the three deep-link sub-files exist for the surrounding *design* narrative (why each sweep moved pre-relocation, what algorithm it runs, its audit-log shape), not as the invocation reference a session reads at execution time. A reader walking `00e` top to bottom hits three subcommand names with no flags, and has to either guess or open three other files mid-sweep to recover what those files already documented in full. The fix restates the same three literal blocks directly in `00e`, at the point they're actually run, rather than requiring the jump.

**Why not move the invocations OUT of the sub-files instead of duplicating them.** The sub-files' own prose (the compound-shape-refusal history, the `--repo-root`-resolved-in-a-separate-call rule, the audit-log field shapes) depends on having the literal block adjacent to explain it — deleting the block from there would break that narrative's own worked example. Duplication is bounded and unlikely to drift silently: `worktree-reap.sh`'s own `usage()` heredoc (reachable via `-h`/`--help` on any subcommand, and the normative source per [#1400](https://github.com/mattsears18/shipyard/issues/1400)) is the actual source of truth if the two documented copies ever disagree — the same division of labor [#716](https://github.com/mattsears18/shipyard/issues/716)'s `detect-ungated-admin-direct-merge.sh` established for a *behavioral* predicate documented in two places (a single executable script instead of two independently-drifting prose copies). Here the "single executable source of truth" is the script's own `usage()` block rather than a helper script, since the thing being restated is a static flag *contract*, not a computed *verdict* — but the anti-drift principle is the same: point every prose copy at one authoritative artifact rather than trusting hand-sync.

**Why `triage-orphan-branches` genuinely needs a different required-flag set, not an oversight to normalize away.** `reap-orphan-orchestrators` and `sweep-stale-agents` only ever touch worktrees the orchestrator's own repo checkout already knows about — `--repo-root` and `--session-id` are sufficient. `triage-orphan-branches` additionally opens/queries PRs (`gh pr create`, `gh pr view`) and diffs each candidate worktree against the default branch (`git rev-list --count origin/<default-branch>..HEAD`), so it needs `--repo` (to scope the `gh` calls) and `--default-branch` (to compute the diff) that neither sibling sweep has any use for. [#1400](https://github.com/mattsears18/shipyard/issues/1400) already closed the *adjacent* ambiguity this asymmetry created — `triage-orphan-branches` now accepts-and-ignores `--session-id` purely for the CLI-symmetry a caller reasonably expects from three sweeps documented together — but accepting the flag doesn't make it required, and requiring `--default-branch`/`--repo` uniformly on the other two sweeps would mean threading unused values through calls that have no work to do with them. The asymmetry is inherent to what each sweep actually does; #1455's fix is to state it plainly at the call site rather than have the caller discover it via a `64`-exit refusal.

### The #1471 command-shape experiment

Issue [#1471](https://github.com/mattsears18/shipyard/issues/1471) reported two mandatory setup blocks — `setup/04-backlog-divert.md`'s step-4 `classify-backlog.sh` invocation and `setup/00-config-worktree.md`'s step-0.5 plugin-root stash read-back — still refused verbatim by the worktree-isolation guard on `main` at 4.46.0, after four prior sweeps had each fixed a different refusal axis. The issue asked for the trigger to be *isolated experimentally* before either block was rewritten, precisely because a fifth guess was the predictable failure mode. This section records the measurements so the next reader inherits the finding instead of re-deriving it.

**Method.** Every command was issued as its own `Bash` tool call from inside a live isolated worktree (`.claude/worktrees/agent-*`), so the guard was active. One variable was toggled at a time. Each verdict was re-tested 2–3× before being recorded. "RUNS" below means the command reached the shell (including reaching a script that then exited non-zero on its own arguments); "REFUSED" means the guard rejected it before execution with *"this command is too complex to verify that it stays inside the worktree."*

**Determinism.** No command changed verdict across repeats. The session that filed #1471 had hypothesised a non-deterministic or stateful guard after observing the same script accepted and then refused ~40 minutes apart; that pair differed in two ways at once (an argument's value *and* a trailing `; echo rc=$?` construct), and the instability does not survive one-variable-at-a-time isolation. **The guard is deterministic**, and the issue body's non-determinism caveat is superseded by this measurement.

**The decisive triple.** These three, taken together, rule out every "compoundness" explanation:

| Command | Verdict |
|---|---|
| 5-statement preamble + `bash "$CLAUDE_PLUGIN_ROOT/scripts/classify-backlog.sh" --help` | RUNS |
| No preamble + full 8-flag `classify-backlog.sh run --repo … --out …`, all literals | RUNS |
| 5-statement preamble + the same invocation carrying `--me "$ME_LOGIN"` | REFUSED |

Neither half is refused alone. What refuses is the *reference to `$ME_LOGIN`* — a variable the block assigns from `ME_LOGIN=$(gh api user --jq '.login')`, a network call whose result the guard cannot statically resolve. Holding the entire block byte-identical and substituting the literal login flips it to RUNS; holding the *literal path* and reintroducing only `--me "$ME_LOGIN"` flips it back to REFUSED. That single argument is the whole difference.

**What this rules out.** Each of these was independently believed, and each is contradicted by a measurement above or below:

- **Line count / multi-statement compoundness.** The 5-statement preamble runs fine with a `--help` or a one-flag invocation.
- **Flag or argument count.** The full 8-flag invocation runs fine with every argument literal, preamble included.
- **Backslash line continuations.** The refused block collapsed to one line is refused identically; the accepted block split across continuations runs identically. Line structure is orthogonal.
- **Assignment-RHS `$(cmd)`** — [#1352](https://github.com/mattsears18/shipyard/issues/1352)'s stated axis. `ME=$(pwd)` runs. `CLAUDE_PLUGIN_ROOT=$(cat .shipyard-plugin-root 2>/dev/null)` followed by `export CLAUDE_PLUGIN_ROOT` and a use of `"$CLAUDE_PLUGIN_ROOT/scripts/…"` runs. #1352's sweep was still worth having as hygiene, but its stated cause does not reproduce as a general rule, and continuing to believe it is part of why these two blocks stayed broken.
- **A `.sh`-shaped token in argument position.** `echo plugins/shipyard/scripts/compound-block-scan.sh` runs.

**One genuinely new syntactic shape, cleanly isolated.** An **unquoted** command substitution with a literal path suffix glued on is refused; quoting it is sufficient:

| Command | Verdict |
|---|---|
| `CLAUDE_PLUGIN_ROOT=$(git rev-parse --show-toplevel)/plugins/shipyard` + use | REFUSED |
| `CLAUDE_PLUGIN_ROOT="$(git rev-parse --show-toplevel)/plugins/shipyard"` + use | RUNS |

Verified across both `export VAR=…` and split `VAR=…` / `export VAR` forms, and across single-line and multi-line rendering — 3 refusals against 4 acceptances, with quoting as the only toggled variable. [`compound-block-scan.sh`](../scripts/compound-block-scan.sh) now detects this shape as its third flagged shape. It is deliberately NOT added to [`command-substitution-scan.sh`](../scripts/command-substitution-scan.sh): that scanner's strict assignment-RHS carve-out treats a `$(` sitting immediately after an `=` as sanctioned, which this shape is, so catching it there would mean weakening a carve-out that exists for good reason.

**Secondary observations** (consistent, lower-stakes): shell special parameters and unset variables refuse when they form a path-shaped word — `echo $?`, `echo $$`, `echo rc=$?`, `echo x$?`, `echo $UNSET_VARIABLE_XYZ`, `echo "$UNSET_VARIABLE_XYZ/foo"`, `echo "$CLAUDE_PLUGIN_ROOT/scripts/compound-block-scan.sh"` (with `CLAUDE_PLUGIN_ROOT` genuinely unset in the process environment, which it always is in a fresh `Bash` call) — but pass inside a `label=value`-shaped word: `echo "rc=$?"`, `echo "x=$?"`, `echo "x:$?"`, `echo "prefix-$?"`. A **set** environment variable resolves fine: `echo $HOME` runs.

**A trap in the refusal message itself.** The message ends *"Run the equivalent from &lt;worktree&gt; without the redirect."* That trailing clause is a **canned suffix, not a diagnosis** — it appears verbatim on `echo $?`, which contains no redirect at all. Reading it as a hint about redirection sends the reader after the wrong variable, and is a plausible contributor to earlier misattributions.

**What remains genuinely unknown, stated so it isn't silently re-guessed.** *Exactly* which command substitutions the guard can statically resolve is not established. In assignment position `$(cat <file-in-worktree>)`, `$(pwd)`, and `$(git rev-parse --show-toplevel)` all behave as resolvable, while `$(gh api …)` does not — plausibly a read-only-and-local versus network distinction, but that was not tested to a conclusion because the fix does not depend on it. **Substituting literals sidesteps the question entirely**, which is why that is the fix rather than a cleverer expansion form. Do not extrapolate a rule from this paragraph without re-running the experiment.

**Why the fix is "substitute literals" and not another decomposition.** By the time either block runs, the orchestrator already holds every value the block was re-deriving: the plugin root (resolved and stashed moments earlier in step 0.5), the primary root (step 0.56's stash), and the `gh` login (one plain call). The preambles existed to carry those values *across* `Bash` calls, since shell variables do not survive between them — but the orchestrator's own context survives perfectly well, and substituting from it removes the variable references that the guard actually objects to. `SHIPYARD_REPO_ROOT` is the one value a child process genuinely needs in its environment, and a per-command environment prefix (`SHIPYARD_REPO_ROOT=<literal> bash …/classify-backlog.sh run …`) exports it to the child exactly as the old two-statement `export` did, in one plain command.

**Deliberately out of scope for #1471's fix.** The issue's own step 4 — folding a "does this fenced block actually *run* post-relocation" assertion into the `spec-script-reference-scan.sh` family — was left unimplemented and filed as a follow-up. A scanner that could answer that question would have to model the guard's resolvability boundary, which the paragraph above states is not established; building it on a guessed boundary would repeat the #1308/#1311/#1314/#1352 pattern one more time. **That follow-up is [#1474](https://github.com/mattsears18/shipyard/issues/1474), and it measured the boundary — see the next section. Several statements in this section are superseded by it**, most importantly the "still genuinely unknown" paragraph's local-vs-network hypothesis (there is no such split) and the framing of the trigger as an unresolvable *variable reference* (it is not about variables at all).

### The #1474 resolvability-boundary measurement — and why no "does this block run" gate was built

Issue [#1474](https://github.com/mattsears18/shipyard/issues/1474) is #1471's deferred step 4, split into a prerequisite and a decision: **first** measure the guard's resolvability boundary experimentally, **then** decide whether a "does this fenced block actually run post-relocation" gate is buildable on the result. The split existed because four prior sweeps (#1308 / #1311 / #1314 / #1352) each shipped a real fix built on a plausible-but-unverified model of the guard, and all four left the two target blocks refused anyway; a fifth model-first attempt was the predictable failure mode. This section records the measurement, then the decision.

**Method.** Identical to #1471's — every command issued as its own `Bash` tool call from inside a live isolated worktree (`.claude/worktrees/agent-*`), one variable toggled at a time, each verdict re-run 2–3×. "RUNS" means the command reached the shell (a non-zero exit from the command *itself* still counts); "REFUSED" means the guard rejected it before execution. No verdict flipped across repeats — **#1471's determinism finding reproduces.**

#### The headline: #1471's "resolvability" framing does not survive isolation

#1471 left open whether the guard could statically resolve *local* command substitutions but not *network* ones, having observed `$(pwd)`, `$(cat <file-in-worktree>)`, and `$(git rev-parse --show-toplevel)` "behaving as resolvable" while `$(gh api …)` did not. **That distinction does not exist.** Holding the use position fixed and varying only the right-hand side:

| Block | Verdict |
|---|---|
| `X=$(pwd)` + `echo "$X"` | REFUSED |
| `X=$(date)` + `echo "$X"` | REFUSED |
| `X=$(cat <file-in-worktree>)` + `echo "$X"` | REFUSED |
| `X=$(git rev-parse --show-toplevel)` + `echo "$X"` | REFUSED |
| `X=$(gh api user --jq '.login')` + `echo "$X"` | REFUSED |
| `X=$(curl -sS -m 3 https://api.github.com/zen)` + `echo "$X"` | REFUSED |

Every command substitution is equally unresolvable — local, network, file-read, or otherwise. **Open question 1 (is local-vs-network the real discriminator?) is answered NO**; it was an artifact of the use position in the blocks #1471 happened to test. **Open question 2 (does the guard read the referenced file to resolve `$(cat …)`?) is answered NO** — it does not resolve values at all.

What #1471 mistook for RHS resolvability was the *use position*. Same RHS, only the use varies:

| Block | Verdict |
|---|---|
| `X=$(gh api user --jq '.login')` + `echo "$X"` | REFUSED |
| `X=$(gh api user --jq '.login')` + `echo "$X/scripts/compound-block-scan.sh"` | RUNS |
| `X=$(pwd)` + `echo "$X"` | REFUSED |
| `X=$(pwd)` + `echo "$X/scripts/compound-block-scan.sh"` | RUNS |

#### The actual predicate

**A command is refused when it contains a word whose ENTIRE content is a single unresolvable expansion.** Not a token, not a shape, not compoundness — a word. "Unresolvable" means the value is not statically knowable; "resolvable" means assigned from a literal earlier in the same block (transitively — `X="a"` + `Y="$X/b"` + `echo "$Y"` RUNS) or present in the process environment (`echo "$HOME"` RUNS bare).

Adding **any** literal text to that same word rescues it:

| Use of an unresolvable `$X` | Verdict |
|---|---|
| `echo "$X"` | REFUSED |
| `echo "$X/foo"` | RUNS |
| `echo "prefix-$X"` | RUNS |
| `echo "value=$X"` | RUNS |
| `echo "[$X]"` | RUNS |
| `echo "$X-suffix"` | RUNS |
| `echo "$X."` | **REFUSED** — see "edges" below |

**It is not about variables at all.** A direct command substitution behaves identically, as a *single* statement with no variable in sight:

| Command | Verdict |
|---|---|
